 Communities and stakeholders will be able to access the  grievance complaint form . Such grievances will be 
raised to SPC as the Accredited Entity as well as to the EPB.  
 
STAKEHOLDER ENGAGEMENT  
171. An initial stakeholder workshop took  place in October 2019. The workshop was conducted between SPC and 
representatives from key stakeholder agencies including the FSM NDA, FSM DECEM, the FSM Overseas 
Development Assistance (ODA) office, the FSM Department of Resources and Development, GCF focal points 
representing each state, the Micronesia Conservation Trust, the IOM office, representatives from the municipalities, 
as well as other relevant organizations ( e.g., local NGOs) (please see Annex I of the SEP for the full report).  
172. Following this workshop, the proposal was developed in close coordination with the FSM NDA and continuous 
consultations have taken place from June to November 2020. Due to precautionary measures and preventive 
practices that were in place to prev ent the spread of COVID -19 such as travel restrictions and mandates prohibiting 
group gatherings, these consultations took place either virtually or with one or two people at a time.  
173. During project development, key women’s groups were also consulted to ensure appropriate gender 
mainstreaming and to validate the gender action plan. The Gender Analysis and Action Plan Annex 4 includes a 
summary of the outcomes of these consultations and how they were integrated into project design.  
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max.  500 words, approximately 1  page )  
174. SPC facilitated extensi ve stakeholder consultations  and data analysis to comprehensively assess the baseline 
in terms of gender gaps and opportunities for gender -inclusive sub -grants preparation and impl ementation. The 
context for each of the sectoral focuses of the EDA Programme was assessed to identify women’s roles and 
responsibilities along with the challenges that they face.  
175. With regards to DRR and coastal protection, t he assessment found that both men and women play a critical 
role in the preparation and recovery process, b ut women may not have the same capacity to influence decision-
making. Men, particularly those with greater levels of power and authority, are usually the ones informed and consulted by response agencies, including governments, and they directly participate in the decision- making and 
management processes for disaster risk management. This could mean that women’s needs,  and priorities are not 
properly addressed in early warning systems, preparedness, and during the recovery process. Further, there is 
evidence of increased violence against women during disaster recovery such as flooding from climate change and 
extreme events.  
176. In terms of water security, results suggest that there is often a clear division of labour  between men and women 
in water resources management. These different roles and responsibilities vary, but in general women are tasked 
with managing household water for drinking, cooking and other uses as well as making sure catchments are clean.  
177. Lastly, with regards to food security, women play a cri tical role in food production both through subsistence 
farming to feed their families as well as growing cash crops for income and cultural ceremonies. Despite this, 
women often face barriers to accessing agricultural land, training, credit and services. T he agricultural production 
that women and girls perform also tends to be considered part of “women’s household responsibilities”. Alongside these challenges, climate change will make it more difficult to make a living from agriculture and women may also 
struggle more than men to find alternative livelihoods, enter the formal employment sector, or migrate due to cultural 
barriers and limited opportunities and education.
  
178. To integrate gender -sensitive planning and considerations , the EDA Programme  will integrate gender 
dimensions into all training and capacity building activities, while target ing at least 50% women in training 
attendees. To address the information gap on gender -sensitive adaptation measures , the EDA Programme  will 
collect and centr alise information and data on the integration of gender -responsiveness in sub- grant design. As a 
result, locally adapted baselines will be developed to promote gender mainstreaming to participating LAs . 
Additionally, SPC will recruit at least two women for  the development of tender documents as part of Activity 2.1.2. 
Lastly, sub-grants will be evaluated using a dedicated Initial Sub- grant Gender Assessment form, which can be 
found in Annex 4.   
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1  page)  
179. Financial control and procurement processes will be implemented as per SPC rules and regulations, which 
were certified as acceptable to the GCF in the SPC accreditation process. SPC has specific policies for financial 
management including “Finance Regulations”, “Anti -money laundering and counter -terrorism financing”, 
“Procurement” and “Grants and sub- delegation” as well as its “Staff Regulations and Manual of Staff Policies” that 
govern HR and other relevant processes and proc edures. These and other policies are available on the SPC


---
*Page 75*
---

G 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 73 OF 75 
   
 Intranet and are adhered to in the implementation of all SPC projects . All entities involved in the Programme’s 
implementation will comply with those aforementioned policies.  
180. When under implementation, financial management will also remain in compliance  with the FSM Financial 
Management Regulations. This will support the ongoing sustainability of the programme as it will already be 
operating within the framework of the rules and regulations that will govern the management of the programme and 
implementation of activities after the GCF -funded period.  
181. SPC’s Financial Management Information System (FMIS)  is a Microsoft Dynamics NAV -based enterprise 
resource planning system that has  been deployed to all of its regional offices, which provides SPC  employees  with 
financ ial management  functionalities. Using FMIS improves the flow of financial information, supports financial 
monitoring and reporting, increases transparency and visibility , and strengthens internal control. SPC maintains 
organization- wide separation of income and expenditure by donor and project so that financial and other data can 
be recorded, classified and summarized to facilitate internal management and external reporti ng requirements.  
182. As mentioned in Section B .4., SPC as the Accredited Entity to the GCF will have overall responsibility for quality 
assurance and oversight of co- executing entities. In addition to this, as the sole executing entity for the project , 
SPC will be responsible for the financial execution of GCF funds according to SPC rules and regulations mainly 
contained and detailed in the SPC Handbook (including those referred to financial monitoring, audit and 
procurement).  
183. During implementation, SPC will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and 
procedures. This may include monitoring missions, spot checks and participation at relevant  meetings. The project 
will be subject to SPC’s  audit regime, including the external audit and internal audit functions  with oversight provided 
by SPC’s independent Audit and Review Committee.  
184. SPC’s CFU will be responsible for oversight of project management and financial monitoring,  compris ing a 
GCF Programme Manager , GCF Programme Officer  and GCF Finance and Administrati ve Assistant.  This team 
will interact with and support the work of the ECU (based within SPC’s MRO) , which includes a Programme 
Coordinator, Programme Administrative Assistant, Finance and Procurement Officer and other part time support 
staff.  
185. The financial and operational management of the sub- grants provided through the RCGF will be undertaken in 
line with the provisions of the Operations Manual (please see Annex 21). Disbursement of funds to successful 
grantees will proceed according to SPC’s Finance Regulations (2018), Finance Policy: Procurement (2020), 
Finance Policy: Grants and Sub- delegations (2020) and Finance Policy: Anti -Money Laundering and Counter -
Terrorism Financing (2020). These policies contain contr ols and due diligence to ensure that funds provided to 
grantees are properly disbursed and managed . Disbursements will be conditional on the achievement of milestones 
as outlined in the grants agreements between the ECU and the grantees. Verification of mi lestones and approv al 
of payment request s will be undertaken by the Grants O fficer based on compliance with grant agreement 
conditions. Annual financial reports will be submitted by grantees for the review of the ECU  concerning all activities 
implemented under the EDA facility for the grantees’ financial years. All reports will be reviewed and formally 
approved by the ECU . Grantees are als o required to keep supporting document ation  detail ing expenses incurred 
in achievement of the grant milestones . Specific a udits of grants will be undertaken based on requests made by 
the ECU , with grantees also expected to share details of their regular au dits150.

redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one 
of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the docume nt contains 
specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Secretariat ’s assessment  of FP169 
Proposal name:  Climate change adaptation solutions for L ocal Authorities

I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat   
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks:  
Strengths  Points of caution  
The proposed enhancing direct access ( EDA ) pilot 
will operate in the Federated States of Micronesia 
(FSM ), a priority country under the EDA request 
for proposals (small island developing States -  
SIDS) and is the first EDA project in the Pacific 
region.  The project pipeline is yet to be developed based 
on the needs identified by the local communities 
with the list of activities being indicative only; this 
is fully compliant with the EDA approach, 
nonetheless it might pose additional operational 
risks that cannot be assessed at the EDA funding 
proposa l appraisal stage.  
Fully compliant with the EDA requirements 
regarding:  
(a) A robust governance system with detailed 
project selection, review and 
implementation arrangements;  
(b) Empowerment and devolution of decision -
making to subnational actors and clear 
adaptation focus at the community level;  and 
(c) A clearly presented Operations Man ual for 
the proposed Resilient Communities Grant 
Facility, (RCGF) which EDA will capitalize 
(component 2) . Component 1 is crucial to ensure the necessary 
capacity of the local authorities and stakeholders that will be involved in the preparation of the 
subprojects. While an in -depth institutional 
capacity assessment of the local institutions was presented in the feasibility  study , there is 
uncertainty about how rapidly all local actors 
could achieve sufficient capacity  to deliver the 
expected pipeline of su bprojects, and this could 
potentially cause delays in implementation.  
Strong country ownership and long -term vision 
from the FSM state and local institutions to 
maintain the RCGF as an open -ended facility 
serving beyond GCF exit.   
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and 
conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and 
recommendations”, respectively.  
II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

*Page 82*

GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 82 
 
 
2.1 Progra mme  background  
3. The Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) is a widely dispersed archipelago located in 
the North Pacific Ocean. FSM is formed by four states (Chuuk, Kosrae, Pohnpei and  Yap) and 
comprise s 607 islands, of which 74 are inhabited. The distance b etween the eastern -most State 
(Kosrae) and the western -most State (Yap) is 2,700  kilometres . Under the 1979 Constitution, 
FSM has three levels of government: national, state, and municipal. The four State G overnments 
of Chuuk, Kosrae Pohnpei, and Yap are r elatively autonomous. Each State has its own governor, 
judiciary and legislative bodies, along with relevant government departments. As of 2019, FSM’s 
estimated population is 113,815, with an annual  GDP of USD 329 million and a GDP per capita of 
USD 3,400. The majority of the country’s population live in the coastal regions of the high 
islands, with more than half the population living in rural areas.  
4. The Pacific Catastrophe and Risk Financing Initiative (PCRAFI) estimates that it is likely 
that FSM will incur on average USD  8 million per year in losses from earthquakes and tropical 
cyclones.1 In the coming 50 years, FSM has a 50  per cent  chance of experiencing natural disaster 
losses exceeding USD  105 million and casualties greater than 220 people. I n addition, there is a 
10 per cent  chance of experiencing a loss exceeding USD  470 million and casualties of more than 
600 people.2 Damages to public infrastructure and private capital as well as drops in total factor 
productivity would cause a loss to GDP , while higher spending on post -disaster rehabilitation 
and reconstruction of damaged infrastructure would lead to higher fiscal deficit and public debt.  
5. This project will overcome critical barriers to strengthen the resilience of vulnerable 
communities to  the impacts of extreme climatic events as well as long -term water and food 
insecurity as a result of predicted climate change. GCF resources will be critical in strengthening 
the adaptive capacity of local authorities and communities to improve disaster r isk reduction 
(DRR)  and coastal management as well as food and water security for the most vulnerable 
populations of FSM located in the main islands and outer atolls.  
6. The EDA grant facility created under this project  and named the Resilient  Communities 
Grant Facility (RCGF)  will directly contribute to increasing the adaptive capacity of local 
communities and is expected to  deliver a number of economic, social, environmental and gender 
co-benefits. The project is anticipated to directly  benefit 45,019 people, accounting for 40  per 
cent  of the total population  of FSM . Indirect beneficiaries of the proposed project are gauged to 
be 100  per cent  of the population.3 
7. Climate objective:  According to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative, FSM is the 
fourth most vulnerable country to climate change and the seventy- eighth  in terms of climate 
change readiness in the world (FSM scored 0.640 on the vulnerability scale and 0.360 on the 
readiness scale). The FSM GCF Country Programme concluded that at present, none of the FSM 
States have the  ‘high’ level of adaptive capacity required to ensure adaptation to the effects of 
climate change. The climate of FSM displays considerable year -on-year variability due to the El 
Niño -Southern Oscillation (ENSO). The country has two seasons – a dry season from November 
to April and a wet season from May to October. Rainfall throughout the country and region is 
affected by the movement of the Intertropical Convergence Zone .4  
 
1 Federated States of Micronesia, Country Risk Profile (2011), Pacific Catastrophe and Risk Financing Initiative.  
2 Lee, D., Zhang, H., and Nguyen, C . 2018. The Economic Impact of Natural Disasters in Pacific Island Countries: 
Adaptation and Preparedness. IMF Working Papers , 18/108, International Monetary Fund, Washington.  
3 The numbers provided represent conservative estimates to be understood as the minimum number of beneficiaries 
across all three thematic areas, although some areas have an estimated highe r number of beneficiaries than the 
weighted average. This methodological choice was made to prevent the possibility of double counting as 
beneficiaries of projects in each state may  overlap.  
4 There are no comprehensive studies on freshwater resources and climate change in FSM, and large data gaps 
remain , especially for hydraulic modelling of atoll aquifer systems.


---
*Page 83*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 83 
 
 
8. Approximately 60  per cent  of households in FSM live within 180 metr es of the shoreline, 
with the State of Kosrae having the greatest proportion of coastal -dwelling households (80 per 
cent ) and Chuuk having the smallest  (38 per cent ). Of the FSM p opulation 94 per cent live within 
at least 1  kilometre  of the coastal strip and 100  per cent  live within 5  kilometres . Additionally, 
the majority of  structures in FSM are located near or along the coast , almost all outer island 
islets lie within the 2 -met re zone of potential sea level rise, and all lie within a 5 -met re zone of 
storm surge. This poses a significant disaster risk to the majority of households in the project 
area. Due to the diversity of environmental conditions between States and their respective 
islands and atolls, climate trends tend to differ from one island S tate to another, and between 
island States and atolls.   
9. The climate hazards that this project seeks  to address are mostly sea level rise, increase 
in temperatures and variability in the precipitation levels, as well as extreme weather events .5  
10. There is variation in air temperature trends within FSM. For the state of Pohnpei, the 
greatest trends involve minimum air temperature, whereas for Yap, the greater trends are 
observed in maximum air temperature. Annual maximum and minimum temperatures in FSM 
have increased since records began in 1952, with annual maximum temperatures having  
increased at a rate of 0.18°C per decade in Pohnpei (eastern FSM) and 0.23ºC per decade in Yap 
(western FSM) .  
11. The ENSO phenomenon has a profound effect on the distribution of tropical cyclones in 
FSM . The anomalous ocean -atmosphere conditions during ENSO can induce changes in the 
intensity, frequency and tracks of storms, thereby affecting the generation of extremes such as tides and storm surges. These events cause marine inundation that damages groundwater 
resources, taro beds, soil, and agro forestry resources in coastal settings, especially on low atoll 
islets. On high islands, coastal communities that experience both intensifying storm runoff and rising ocean waters are experiencing increased flooding and other drainage problems . 
12. Financing i nformation  and  environmental and social safeguards ( ESS)  category : 
The total programme cost  is USD 19,790,637 comprising USD 16,591,556 of GCF funding in the 
form of grants and USD 3,199,081 co -finance from the Government of FSM and SPC  in the form 
of in -kind contributions , and the ESS category  of this project is levelled at  B.  
2.2 Component -by-component analysis 
13. Component 1 is structured to address barriers to respond to projected climate impacts 
by the local communities and municipalities, as well as identify and prepare the project pipeline , 
while Component 2 is aimed at establishing the financial flows and access through the creation 
of the Resilient Communities Grant F acility  (RCGF)  to support  the protection and resilience of 
vulnerable communities.  
Component 1: Local authorities empowered to deliver climate change adaptation services to their 
population (GC F: USD 1,724,637 ; FSM: USD 70,000)  
14. Under this component, the accredited entity ( AE) will develop an adaptation measure 
prioritization framework, climate risk screening tools and a training curriculum informed by scientific data and analysis that will support LAs to identify and screen for climate change 
adaptation risks and identify appr opriate adaptation measures. In addition, a ctivities under this 
component  will include develop ment  of a climate adaptation decision support framework and 
training curriculum targeting mostly government officials  at the local level.  SPC will train local 
authorities ( LAs) on the framework and resources identified and provide an introduction to the 
 
5 This conclusion is based on analysis of daily rainfall data from a subset of Coupled Model Intercomparison Project 5

Industrial Research Organisation ( CSIRO ) (2011) .

enhancing di rect access (EDA) f acility to familiarize local government officials with the 
objectives of the facility, the process, eligibility criteria, and details of the three thematic areas 
for adaptation sub -grants. The AE will develop a train -the-trainer’s module  and conduct training 
for national consultants and/or local non- governmental organizations ( NGOs ) in each State to 
become Facilitating Agents, who will then be responsible for supporting the L As to prioritize 
adaptation actions for the f acility  and train LAs on how to develop adaptation sub -grants .  
15. Lastly, k nowledge -sharing mechanisms to develop and foster a network of local 
government authorities will also be supported under this component, and will include learning 
exchanges and site visits.  A web portal  will be developed together with the database and will 
include sex -disaggregated data at the municipal and subproject level on the three thematic 
areas and on key socioeconomic indicators to inform the design of future adaptation 
interventions.  
Component 2: Priority project implementation : Establis hment of the Resilient Communities Grant 
Facility for enhancing local community resilience . (GCF: USD 13,943,717 ; SPC: USD 91,750 ; FSM: 
USD 2,887,571 )  
16. Under this component, the AE will establish a Resilient Communities Grants Facility 
(RCGF) to address the long -term vulnerabilities identified  in the f unding proposal . Under this 
output, the Facility w ill openly and competitively fund applications for adaptation measures  to 
be submitted by the LAs . It will be established and function as a grants funding mechanism to be 
governed as set out in the Operations Manual developed by the AE and included in this f unding 
proposal. The RCGF will provide sub -grants for prioritized  activities identified  under 
Compo nent 1. The RCGF will award grants and provide targeted technical assistance to LAs and 
State Governments across all four FSM States , including outer atolls. It will support 30–40 sub -
grants in the range of USD 75,000 –USD 1,000,000. An estimated breakdown of size of projects is 
provided in table 1 below:

17. In addition to building climate resiliency, the EDA f acility, through the awarded grants, 
will assist in strengthening the local managerial and financial as well as monitoring, evaluation 
and learning capacities so that the recipient LAs develop sufficient capability to implement 
adaptation sub -grants and su stain the country’s climate -resilient development in the long term.  
18. The robust governance of this project, which includes a set of steps for proposal 
selection, review and approval,  ensures that the projects that are most technically sound and 
well aligned with the GCF investment criteria will be financed by the f acility. The project 
selection will be based on the following steps: (i) submitting the expression of i nterest ; (ii) 
reviewing and shortlisting the projects by the  SPC Implementation Unit ; (iii) invitations to the 
LAs to submit project proposals, which may also result in technical support provided for project 
preparation ; (iv) screening of the f unding proposals by the Grants Technical Evaluation 
committee , which wil l provide an advisory report on both technical and financial sides of the 
proposals ; and (v) presenting the projects to the Board for approval. The expression of interest  
process will allow the project management unit  to pre -screen sub -grants against the G CF 
investment criteria, eligibility under the EDA f acility’s three thematic areas, environmental and 
social  risk level, climate reasoning and rationale,  and level of community input/engagement .


---
*Page 85*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 85 
 
 
19. Awarded grants will be implemented, monitored and evaluated. To ensure LAs have the 
required project management skills necessary for the implementation of sub -grants, targeted 
project management training will be provided.  
20. Lastly, during this implementation  phase, the AE will engage with development partners 
to identify resources for re -capitalization of the EDA fund, preparing a resource mobilization 
strategy and preparing and securing application funding requests.  The resource mobilization 
activities will include assessing the extent to which regular government expenditure for 
resilience -building activities might be redirected into the RCGF. For example, this would allow 
for funds from the Department of Environment, Climate Change and Emergency Management t o 
be funnelled through the RCGF to achieve the objectives of the Department’s annual workplans. 
Similar engagement with State -level governments will be undertaken. Other activities for 
resource mobili zation will include undertaking analyses to determine th e minimum amount of 
capitalization needed for the viability of phase 2 of the RCGF based on projects under 
implementation as well as scenario planning based on future needs and a capacity assessment 
to determine the optimal extent of funding to scale up th e RCGF.  
21. One of the innovative aspects of this proposal is that under C omponent 2, the project 
will also work with local communities. The i nvolvement of the local communities , such as NGOs 
or other society organizations , in this project  is ensured through t heir p articipation and  
involvement as F acilitating Agents for project preparation and support . Facilitating Agents  will 
be selected through the competitive bidding process and will be assigned to each sub -grant, 
whereby they will conduct a site visit once each quarter to support the reporting and 
monitoring processes.  
III. Assessment  of performance  against investment criteria  
3.1 Impact potential                                                                                 Scale: N/A 
22. In terms of the adaptation core indicator, the project has  significant potential at national 
level, as the total number of direct beneficiaries  envisaged  stands at around 40 per cent  of the 
entire country population. Moreover, the intervention has a wides pread scope : all of the 
country’s 75 municipalities will be targeted by the RCGF -supported subprojects , including those 
located in the remote outer islands. As the exact nature and scale of the sub -grants to be 
supported by the RCGF  will be determined at  the implementation stage, estimates for the 
number of direct beneficiaries to be targeted by the p rogramme rest  on the assumption  that at 
least 80  per cent  of the local communities  and local authorities (LAs)  will be targeted for 
different climate adaptation impact  areas  (DRR, ecosystems, food security and water) . This 
assumption look s realistic based on the experience of SPC and FSM in this type of locally -
focused adaptation intervention.  
23. The RCGF will provide grants to subprojects that address at the local level DRR and 
coastal protection adaptation measures . These activities aim at addressing the impacts of a 
changing climate on the livelihoods of the coastal vulnerable population . The specific climate 
relevance of the subprojects financed by the RCGF will be appraised by the SPC project 
management  unit  (ECU) which will administer the RCGF, ensuring that the GCF EDA financing 
will support the total or incremental cost of climate interventions and that the financial resources provided focus on cost -efficiency in achiev ing climate impacts at the local level.  
24. One of the strong point s of the project is the emphasis o n ensuring technical assistance 
to the local authorities and subproject proponents, to ensure that the locally -led intervention 
supported by the RCGF are designed through ( i) specific stakeholders’  consu ltation, including 
the involvement of women, girls and vulnerable groups; ( ii) the information provided by locally -
focused vulnerability assessments; (iii) attention to the avoidance of a lock -in effect and


---
*Page 86*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 86 
 
 
maladaptation ; and ( iv) a focus on empowering loc al institutions and actors to unleash their full 
involvement as the first driver of transformational changes.  
25. Overall, the capacity of the project to delive r the outputs under Component 1 ( capacity -
building and empowerment of local stakeholders) will greatly influence the full extent of the 
impact of th is EDA intervention .  
3.2 Paradigm shift potential                                                          Scale: N/A 
26. The feasibility study present s a theory of change  for the intervention which is logically 
connected to the GCF Results Impact Areas. The theory of change  reflects the focus of this  EDA 
pilot and its sub -grants are centred  on empowering local communities and States to develop 
and tailor climate change projects based on their specific needs and priorities. The three macro 
impact areas of intervention  – DRR, food security and water security  – are identified by the 
need s asses sments carried out by the stakeholder consultations and the prioritization sectors 
identified in the FSM national adaptation plans. These  are linked with the strengths, 
weaknesses, opportunities and threats analysis that identified barriers and the opportu nities 
that the proposed EDA pilot intends to build upon . This is well  explained, but the role of the 
private sector and academia could have emerged more clearly , as well as their involvement as 
active actors to be encompassed during the implementation.  
27. Moreover, in terms of paradigm shift potential the proposed EDA p ilot can:  
(a) Catalyse long -term climate impact and resources beyond the GCF implementation stage . 
The RCGF is designed as a potential open -ended facility with a handover plan for the 
FSM Government. As part of the sustainability plan, there is a resource mobilization 
strategy which intends to coordinate long -term climate donor efforts (beyond the GCF) 
into a coordinated space provided by the RCGF ;  
(b) Build long -term sustainability: the FSM G overnment is strongly supporting the project, 
and it  clearly  emerged that they intend  to sustain the RCGF b eyond the GCF initial 
implementation stage , both as the ultimate owner of the RCGF at the handover phase 
and as coordinator of  a potential donors’ pool , constituting an  open -ended facility , once 
the GCF implementation stage approaches phase -out;  
(c) Scale up t he proposed  approach , to build adaptation, resilience and climate prosperity at 
community level, through a bottom -up approach guided by the local authorities within 
FSM. Also, the overall approach proposed can be meaningfully replicated in other  Pacific 
SIDS , that present similar social , economic and geographical conditions and  the 
presence of climate vulnerable communities residing in outer islands ;  
(d) Creat e an enabling environment: Component 1 of the intervention hinges significantly 
on empoweri ng the local authorities and actors that will be involved in the design and 
implementation of the project  and in the transfer  of knowledge and skills which will be 
pivotal in the design and implementation of the subprojects ; and   
(e) Support the behavioural ch ange of the local institutions and communities involved in 
the project, given the strong bottom -up approach and focus in transferring skills and 
knowledge.  
28. From the point of view of technological innovation, the solutions described in the 
feasibility study  (DRR measures , rainwater catchment systems, use of endemic species,  etc.) are 
known to be effective in producing local -level adaptation results , and some activities , such as 
those that are proposed for agriculture and ecosystem management (mangroves) will build on 
indigenous knowledge.  Overall, it is expected that t echnical knowledge will be generated during 
execution of the sub -grants through the implementation of i nnovative technology solutions to 
identified local constraints. This knowledge will be utilized for selecting appropriate


---
*Page 87*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 87 
 
 
technologies and approaches in other Pacific communities and will help tailor them to specific 
community needs. SPC will also aggregat e and synthesiz e the lessons learned to help drive other 
projects in their portfolio and operations across the Pacific .  
29. In terms of knowledge -generation it is positively noted that this EDA pilot aims to build 
the capacity of local actors  to understand cl imate change impacts to develop and implement 
their own priority adaptation projects. These efforts will drive community ownership and buy -
in while demonstrating the long -term sustainability of these investments, particularly as it 
relates to long -term mai ntenance (i.e. green/blue/grey coastal infrastructure, rainwater 
systems, etc.) and behaviour change (i.e. fisheries and livestock practices).  
3.3 Sustainable development potential                                              Scale:  N/A 
30. The proposed EDA pilot will contribute to the achievement of several Sustainable 
Development Goals , in particular Goals 1,2, 3 and 5.  There are potential economic, social and 
environmental  co-benefits . As the facility  will implement  a broad range of activities at the 
subpro ject level, a large range of co-benefits  are related to DRR, water and food security. F rom 
DRR activities , co-benefits will mostly include  avoided economic losses through enhanced 
disaster resilience and improved recovery timelines . In addition, t he DRR ac tivities are expected 
to lead to improved health outcomes from reduced disaster risk, particularly flooding. Further 
by reducing disaster risk and improving response times, economic and social  community 
recovery can be improved  and recover y from the impact of the Covid19 pandemic  can be 
supported . Finally, DRR activities can also help to preserve cultural sites and resources. 
Watershed management and restoration activities will help to improve water quality, ecosystem 
biodiversity, integrity  and coastal resources.  
31. Social co -benefits from food security activities will focus on improved nutritional 
outcomes, especially for young children, and food security for vulnerable households . The 
activities focused on supporting improved coastal resource  conservation and sustainable 
utilization will help to sustain biodiversity and improve  local habitat.  
32. Economic co -benefits are expected from the enhanced agricultural  productivity and 
reduced disaster losses arising from climate  hazards, as well as the cr eation of jobs from several 
of the subprojects that deal with small -scale infrastructure s and local services.  
33. The intervention has a strong emphasis on gender equality and the inclusion in the 
intervention of the vulnerable segment of the population.  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                                      Scale: N/A 
34. FSM is classified by as low  to middle income SIDS ,6 with over 40  per cent  of the 
population below the nationally determined poverty line .7 It is noted that the EDA p ilot intend 
to prioritize the local authorities whose populations are the most vulnerable  economically and 
most exposed to the effects of climate change.  
35. The type of activities that the RCGF can finance are non -revenue generating and the 
fiscal capacity of the G overnment to support the vulnerable population by provid ing essential 
public goods to support climate adaptation is inherently limited.  
36. The barrier analysis presented in the f unding proposal highlighted a number of capacity 
constraints for FSM.  FSM local, State and national financial and technical capacity is extremely 
 
6 UN Department o f Economic  and Social Affairs. Available at: < https://publicadministration.un.org/egovkb/en -
us/Data/Country- Information/id/111 -Micronesia -Federated -States -of>

constrained, particularly when considering the magnitude of potential climate change impacts. 
Despite some variation in their adaptive capacities, all States are highly vulnerable due mainly 
to an inability to respond to climate impacts in a timely manner and the wide dispersion of the 
islands in FSM which poses transportation, communication and development challenges for the 
nation, particularly for costs of goods and services, costs of energy and transportation, and 
scalability and connectivity of markets.8 Specifically, a previous capacity assessment for FSM by 
USAID9 highlighted that estimated  costing for operationalizing climate change policies and 
action at the State and local levels  currently far outweighs department budgets and that there is 
limited capacity in national and State G overnments to effectively coordinate climate finance and 
adaptation priorities . The total financing required for the identified pipeline of projects and 
programmes is estimated at  USD 1.4 billion  which exceeds FSM’s national GDP five -fold.  
37. Furthermore , the activities proposed are relevant to overcome the financial and capacity 
barriers identified by SPC and FSM in the funding proposal, and the EDA approach proposed  
will further tailor the activities based on the involvement of local actors in the desig n stage of 
each subproject . This approach can further increase the relevance of the proposed intervention 
for the individuals and households  that will be involved  in the subprojects.  
3.5 Country ownership                                                                      Scale: N/A 
38. The intended nationally determined contribution of FSM emphasizes the urgency of  
enhanc ing local capacity to plan, design, implement, manage and operate adaptation projects 
and installed technologies, further demonstrating the relevance of the proposed EDA pilot.  
39. The programme will work to shift the status quo from a pathway of climate 
vulnera bility, elevated health risks and limited socioeconomic development for vulnerable 
communities in FSM to one of improved food/water security, enhanced DRR and recovery, and 
improved socioeconomic development by building local capacity to respond to climate  change. 
The EDA pilot has been designed to deliver on the adaptation goals set out in the FSM National 
Climate Change Strategy and contribute to four of the GCF adaptation results areas .  
40. The GCF Country Programme also specifically identifies the need for  food and water 
security projects, disaster risk management projects, and resilient infrastructure/road 
projects .10 Building on identified priorities, and given the centrality of climate change 
considerations, the GCF Country Programme endorses the sectoral focus of the Joint State 
Action Plan  – food and water security, health and education, and infrastructure climate proofing. 
Further, the Country Programme acknowledges the difficulty in leveraging private sector 
financing for climate adaptation projects in  FSM, due to the limited capabilities of national 
private sector actors.  
41. The accredited entity, SPC, have a relevant track record in implementing community -
driven climate project s in FSM, and has partnered with the relevant actors involved in the 
implemen tation of this EDA p ilot at national and local level.  
42. The FSM n ational design ated authority has been strongly involved in the design of the 
project and will have a pivotal role as the Chair  of the EDA Programme Board.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness         Scale:  N/A  
43. The project seeks the maximum possible level of concessionality in accordance with 
FSM’s economic, institutional and fiscal constraints and specificities. Although the Government 
of FSM is taking actions to build a policy an d legal framework to enable climate change action, 
structural barriers to accessing finance remain. Public resources, which are largely dependent 
on external development assistance support , are currently insufficient to achieve the 
meaningful reductions in  climate change vulnerability and to improve resilience of LAs and 
beneficiary populations. Therefore the concessionality requested is gauged as appropriate.  
44. An overall economic analysis of the EDA pilot has been performed and it presents a 
positive  net present value and e conomic internal rate of r eturn . 
45. This proposed EDA model has the potential to catalyse long -term additional financing 
for FSM and its LAs, and a resource mobilization strategy has been developed as an output of 
Component 2. Furthermore, the Government has also submitted commitment letters outlining 
its intention to hand  over the facility post project closure, and continue its efforts to secure 
funding for the facility after its initial capitalization is over. T hrough its focu s on capacity -
building for LAs to develop and implement projects, developing a fundable track record of project execution, and crucial capacity/experience in effective project and financial 
management , the likelihood of new financial sources coming into th e facility is potentially high. 
This can help build donor/investor confidence, reduce investment risk, and ultimately unlock 
future projects and investment for FSM and the LAs.  
46. There are no specific risks to crowd -out private sector investments, given the 
intrinsically public- good nature of the activities that the RC GF will finance . By im prov ing the 
overall economic  conditions and livelihoods of a significant part of the FSM population, the 
project has the potential to favour market creation and local entrepreneurs.  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards   
47. Programme backgro und. This EDA programme intends to strengthen the resilience of 
populations that are vulnerable to the impacts of extreme climate events through empowering 
local authorities in delivering climate change adaptation services to strengthen the resilience of 
their respective constituents and establishing a grant facility to support the implementation of 
adaptation projects. Activities implemented under the programme aim to mitigate the impacts 
of flooding that will result in environmental co -benefits of avoided  soil loss, improved soil 
structure, improved recharge rate of groundwater reserves, and overall enhanced ecosystem 
functions and services across various agroecological zones. S ocial co -benefits include improved 
access to and quality of water for vulnerable households through new catchment, pumping and 
distribution assets, as well as reduced morbidity related to waterborne disease and unsafe 
drinking water.  
48. Environmental and s ocial risk category and safeguard instruments . The project is 
categorized as medium level of intermediation (I -2) as i t involves financing to sub- grant 
activities of local authorities for DRR and food and water security adaptation technologies and practices. These are expected to have mostly minimal environmental and social (E&S) risks with 
some having potential limited adverse E&S risks and impacts. The Secretariat confirms this 
categorization and that it is within the E&S risk accreditation level of the AE . The programme 
has prepared an environmental and social management system (ESMS) as the safeguards 
instrument for this proposal.


---
*Page 90*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 90 
 
 
49. Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards. The 
discussions below describe how the project complies with GCF ESS standards:  
(a) ESS 1 : assessment and management of environmental and social risks and 
impacts . The AE conducted an initial risk assessment on the potential priority 
adaptation measures that will be implemented under the programme and has assessed 
them to be generally expected to have limi ted adverse E&S  risks and/or impacts. The 
potential environmental impacts include the possibility for excessive drawdown from 
aquifers due to insufficient recharge time that could lead to negative impacts on the 
quantity and quality of freshwater due to ov erpumping; impact on local and migratory 
fish stocks and marine environment; erosion and soil degradation due to excavations as 
a result of construction activities; nuisance noise and air pollution; and generation of 
electronic and hazardous waste. Potenti al social impacts result ing from  programme 
implementation may include  the exacerbation of gender inequalities as a result of 
income opportunities that will be created;  and impacts on cultural resources and 
heritage as well as on health and safety of worker s. The ESMS provides guidance and 
procedures that can be used to assess and mitigate these potential impacts. Specific sub -
projects will likewise be required to undergo a detailed environmental and social impact 
assessment (ESIA) and develop an environment al and social management plan (ESMP) 
to mitigate site -specific E&S risks and impacts that will be identified.  
(b) ESS 2 : labour and working conditions . Activities under the programme are expected 
to be small- scale and the physical works are not expected to hav e major occupational 
health and safety concerns. Furthermore, the programme aims to promote non -
discrimination and equal opportunity for work and would comply with national labo ur 
requirements and hiring practices including promoting protection of the fund amental 
rights of workers as guided by the International Labo ur Organization (ILO) conventions.  
(c) ESS 3 : resource efficiency and pollution prevention . The programme will have some 
infrastructure -related activities under the climate -induced DRR and coastal protection 
sub-grants. These include structures such as for cyclone proofing and wave breakers 
that could potentially have localized and temporary im pacts on water, air and land 
quality during construction phase , which will have appropriate mitigation measures. 
The programme is expected to utilize natural resources,  including water and energy , and 
promote sustainable use of resources through promoting the implementation of technology solutions like installing solar rooftop s and  solar water pumps and 
establishing rainwater harvesting systems, among others.  
(d) ESS 4 : community health, safety and security . The programme essentially aims to 
support adaptation interventions that are expected to improve the health, safety and 
security of local communities. Nevertheless, minor impacts are expected to be experienced by nearby communities during construction of these adaptation 
interventions. These includ e potential for nuisance noise and localized air pollution. 
Establishment of the physical interventions will also be evaluated as regards 
infrastructure and equipment design and safety following good international industry 
practices.  
(e) ESS 5 : land acquisitio n and involuntary resettlement . The programme categorically 
excludes supporting any activity that will result in involuntary resettlement. 
Furthermore, mechanisms and stakeholder engagement processes will be in place to 
ensure that the sub -grants do not re sult in involuntary resettlement. Th ese include 
having committees and subcommittees to identify involuntary resettlement risks and exclude such proposals from being financed under the programme.  
(f) ESS 6 : biodiversity conservation and sustainable management o f living natural 
resources. Some activities of the programme could potentially lead to loss of marine


---
*Page 91*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 91 
 
 
and terrestrial habitats as a result of construction of the adaptation infrastructure. 
However, given the size of the sub -grants, potential negative impac ts are expected to be 
limited. Mitigation measures will likewise be developed in the conduct of the ESIA. 
Activities could also lead to introduction of invasive species that may affect the 
biodiversity of the area. However, this risk is considered low and the programme will 
promote the use of non -invasive species in relation to local environmental context and 
implement biosecurity protocols. The risk of introduction of invasive species vis -à-vis 
local biodiversity and environmental conditions will likewise be monitored.  
(g) ESS 7 : indigenous peoples and GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. The AE assessment is 
that there will be low risk of negative impacts to indigenous peoples and local communities and that the proposal is designed to provide funds directly to the most 
vulnerable. Within the gender action plan (GAP) and stakeholder consultations, the AE 
demonstrates ways to promote engagement and participation of communities. Consultations undertaken included community chiefs and traditional leaders.  
(h) ESS 8 : cultural  heritage. The programme intends to support traditional cultural 
practices in its interventions as regards food and water security sub -grants through the 
conduct of iterative participatory stakeholder consultations. The programme will likewise not directly  or indirectly fund activities (as part of its exclusionary criteria) that 
would disrupt or adversely affect an archaeological site or a property of historic or cultural significance.  
50. Implementation arrangements . The AE will set  up an ECU to implement the ESMS and 
ensure that the ESIAs and relevant plans and arrangements for safeguards implementation, 
monitoring, supervision and reporting are complied with. Various bodies and committees will 
also be established to serve as policy and governing structure fo r the programme with local 
authorities and facilitating agents as implementors of the sub -grants. Safeguards specialists will 
be engaged at various levels and will include a pool of external and national E&S experts to 
provide guidance on E&S issues includ ing on implementation review and monitoring.  
51. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure. The programme has 
conducted stakeholder workshops with representatives from key stakeholder agencies,  
including government offices, municipalities and other relevant organizations such as civil 
societ y and local NGOs. It has likewise prepared a stakeholder engagement plan where 
information about the programme will be disclosed to various stakeholders who will be engaged throughout the programme life  cycle. Respo nsible entities for stakeholder engagement 
activities, methods, timing and reporting are also indicated.  
52. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM). The programme will establish a programme-
level GRM in addition to the already existing GRM of the AE. The GRMs will be established to 
receive any concerns or grievances from any affected community as regards E&S  performance 
of any of the activities of the programme. These mechanisms will be accessible, and the 
communities and stakeholders will be able to raise such grie vances to the AE as well as to the 
sub-grant level GRMs that will be established. In line with the Indigenous Peoples Policy, the 
Secretariat’s indigenous peoples’ focal point will be available for assistance at all stages, 
including before a claim has bee n made.  
4.2 Gender policy   
53. The AE has provided a gender assessment and GAP and therefore complies with the 
requirements of the GCF G ender Policy.  
54. The gender assessment provides the context of the enabling environment that will 
enhance gender equality and women’s empowerment in FSM . The country ratified the 
Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination a gainst Women in 2004 , the


---
*Page 92*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 92 
 
 
United  Nations  Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1993 and the U nited Nations  
Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities in 2016. In 2019, the FSM States drafted 
gender -based violence (GBV) action plans under the national “Policy on the Eliminat ion of 
Violence Against Women and Girls ”. The FSM Strategic Development Plan (2004 –2023) further 
highlights seven specific strategic goals for addressing gender and vulnerable groups as part of 
country -level development . The “National Gender Policy ”, endor sed in 2018, aspires to promote 
gender equity, equality, social justice and sustainable development in the country. The Gender 
Development Office is a lead institution and is responsible for gender issues in the country. In 
the agriculture sector, one of t he seven guiding principles of the food security and agriculture 
planning and implementation is “social and gender equity” with one goal of “Improving farm 
incomes and livelihoods with particular focus on gender and vulnerable groups.”  
55. The gender assessme nt was compiled based on a desk review as well as consultations 
with women’s groups in each of the four S tates in FSM . It indicates that despite the existence of 
the enabling environment, women continue to face significant barriers. Social and cultural 
barriers that consider women’s primary role as homemakers and caregivers to children, the 
elderly and people with special needs create lack of alternative livelihoods for women outside of homebased agriculture. This is compounded by barriers to access ing agri cultural land, training, 
credit and social and financial services. Gender -blind design of options and policies for climate 
adaptation and agriculture continue to disadvantage women due to exclusion of women from 
decision -making and governance at a number o f levels. Around 30 per  cent of households live 
below the basic poverty line, and households that are headed by women are more likely to live 
in poverty and earn less than male -headed households. Women have less income earning 
opportunities than men due to  hiring preferences in the formal sector, and the difficulties 
women face in establishing and sustaining micro and small business ventures. Overall, the number of women who earn wages or salary in FSM is less than half that of men, and men have 
higher rates of employment in all fields, including agroforestry. Economic activities in FSM 
consist primarily of subsistence farming and fishing and , in these sectors, almost 50  per cent  of 
men in the labo ur force are considered skilled, compared to less than 20 per cent  of women. 
Women in FSM are absent from the legislative and executive levels of government and continue 
to be significantly underrepresented in civic affairs. It is also concerning that there is a high 
prevalence of GBV and the vast majority of women who experience abuse do not access formal 
support services or authorities and access to services is also difficult.  
56. Almost all FSM households grow crops for consumption, barter and sale. Women play a 
critical role in food production through subsistence farming to feed their families, as well as 
growing cash crops for income. There are differences between the kinds of crops traditionally 
grown by women versus men, as well as differences in the location of where women and men 
farm. Men tend to work on higher ground. This is significant given that the impacts of climate 
change (i.e. saltwater inundation, flooding and coastal erosion, soil fertility , etc.) are most 
pronounced in  planted  areas close to houses, near streams and coastlines. The asses sment has 
demonstrated the differentiated gender relation s, roles and responsibilities in F SM and 
particularly as they  relate to the farming households. The assessment has been able to identify 
the barriers to equal access and benefit from  the GCF intended  investment. It has on the other 
hand also identified and included in the assessment existing potentials that will further support 
the promotion of gender equality and women’s empowerment. This will be done through the 
identification, assessment and planned engagement of women’s groups and their existing 
capacity in the four  main territories to support the project in mainstreaming gender, engaging 
with the Women Development O ffice and leveraging the support of the United Nations Entity for 
Gender Equality and the Empowerment of Women ( UN Women) to address GBV issues.  
57. The GAP provides activities that address the challenges faced by women and includes 
baseline  (0), indicators, targets  (30 per cent ), timelines , budgets  and gender expertise (gender 
expert and E SS/gender equity and social inclusion expert).  The project includes sex 
disaggregated collection of key indicators, inclusive consultations of women and other


---
*Page 93*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 93 
 
 
traditionally marginalized groups, equitable targets for capacity -building with 50  per cent  
targe ts for women’s participation, and outputs that include the development and 
implementation of a gender mainstreaming training module. Activities included are aimed at 
increasing women’s leadership and decision -making in the agriculture sector; increasing 
economic opportunities and monitoring increases in income; strengthening women’s representation in institutional decision -making and coordination; develop ing gender -
responsive and inclusive agriculture and climate change policy ; and ensur ing gender-responsive 
and gender -balanced capacity -building and training are conducted. In addition, activities 
include measures to enhance the food security of vulnerable households (with a focus on 
female -headed households) by leveraging men and women farmers’ knowledge of inputs and 
production systems to better develop climate -smart agriculture practices and seeds, while 
ensuring men and women have equal access to project benefits and participat ion in decision -
making.  
58.  The GAP also includes measures to strength en climate -resilient value  chains and 
market linkages across the agriculture sector and ensure market development takes into 
account differentiated access and availability for men and women farmers, particularly with 
regards to social barriers for access. Activities are designed to empower communities, 
particularly through women’s groups , to promote local consumption of food and ensure food 
processing and preservation techniques are reflective of time, capacity and technological constraints of household far mers particularly women. Since GBV has been identified as a critical 
challenge, the partnership and utilization of already existing tools of UN  Women will contribute 
to a better understanding of violence against women and equip personnel working on the project with ways to reduce GBV. The project has developed measures to ensure that women 
have equal access to the grievance mechanism that will provide all female beneficiaries with a 
clear process for providing comment and raising grievances and ensure women  can report 
grievances anonymously through the establishment of focal points for the project within the 
four  State -level umbrella women’s groups.  
59. In general, the GAP includes measures designed to respond to gender issues, reduce 
gender gaps and support pr ogress towards more gender equality. Women’s access to land is an 
issue that is identified in the gender analysis and the AE is recommended to explore potential opportunities to address the issue of land access and land tenure and incorporate into the GAP 
as appropriate.  
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.  Overall  programme  assessment  (medium risk ) 
60. The project aims to improve food and water security, disaster risk reduction and 
socioeconomic development by strengthening the adaptive capacity of l ocal authorities (LAs) to 
respond to cl imate change. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 16.5 million. The 
Government of FSM and the AE are providing in -kind contribution of USD  2.9 million and USD 
0.2 million, respectively. With GCF grant, the AE will  provide sub -grants to LAs through the EDA 
programme, to implement climate change adaptation activities.  
61. The FSM is a SIDS. The project activities have high socio -environmental benefits but low 
cash returns and limited potential to attract private sector financing. Therefore, the AE is 
requesting a full grant for this project as little potential for revenue generation is envisioned.  
4.3.2.  Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute (low risk)  
62. SPC, as a direct access AE, will serve as both AE and executing entity for this project. 
Headquartered in New Caledonia, it has been operating in the Pacific over 70 years. Its regional


---
*Page 94*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 94 
 
 
office in FSM has been implementing a number of climate change adaptation projects financed 
by external donors.   
4.3.3.  Programme -spe cific risks  (medium risk ) 
63. Duplication with other project activities : There have been a number of past and 
ongoing projects including a GCF -financed project ( “Climate resilient food security for farming 
households across the Federated States of Micronesia” – SAP020) which tried to address similar 
climate change challenges in FSM. Given the small size of the country, it is important to 
coordinate among implementing agencies to avoid the overlapping of activities and outputs. The 
analysis of complementarity an d additionality of this project has been provided in the FP. In 
addition, the eligibility criteria for investments should be included in the term sheet to ensure 
the clear focus of the project.   
64. Implementation in remote locations : The AE identified the geographic remoteness of 
certain LAs as one of the risks as some are very far from main islands and in remote locations. 
This also often associates with the high cost of travel and logistics. Some comfort can be derived 
from the AE’s e stablished procurement protocol and experiences that will adjust the 
transportation needs according to the situation. The AE could serve as a financial and procurement service provider and directly support project implementation in collaboration 
with the L As.  
65. Land ownership:  The project identified traditional social structures related to land 
ownership as one of the implementation barriers. As some project interventions may limit 
access to resources, a conflict of interest may arise during the project impl ementation. The AE 
clarified that the land ownership issue will be addressed differently in different States of FSM, 
providing descriptions about the final authority and established procedures to deal with the 
issue in each State. In addition, the p rogramm e will comply with FSM government legislation 
and established practices with regard to land ownership and tenure for sub -grants.  
66. Economic analysis and viability of the project : Given the nature of the EDA 
programme, the economic analysis is based on the mo st probable interventions that would 
reflect the imminent adaptation needs such as water management, agriculture and coastal 
protection. The economic benefits are expected from the saved cost of water, increased yields 
from climate resilient crops and avoi ded loss from coastal management. The analysis results in 
the economic internal rate of return  of 8 per cent . The project is planning to hand over the EDA 
facility to the G overnment of FSM after completion of the GCF funding period. It will include a 
resou rce mobilization strategy and action plan for the recapitalization of the EDA facility. 
However, the G overnment of FSM is unable to make a commitment to recapitalization of the 
facility due to the country’s dependency on the U nited States  Compact of Free A ssociation 
funding. There is limited potential for allocation of taxes and engaging the private sector given 
the public nature of the project activities.   
4.3.4.  Compliance risk  (medium risk ) 
67. The Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) are not subject to targeted United Nations 
Security Council (UNSC) resolutions.  
68. The Pacific Community (SPC), as accredited entity (AE), confirm s that no individual or 
entity that is listed on any UNSC sanctions list, including the UN Consolidated Sanctions list, will 
be invo lved in any manner with the project or its activities, either as a counterparty, 
implementer, or beneficiary. Furthermore, no project activities will be undertaken in any jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by UNSC resolutions.  
69. SPC advises that, at this stage, there has been no due diligence assessment of 
counterparties as they have not yet been identified. SPC has deemed it more appropriate to


---
*Page 95*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 95 
 
 
follow a fair, competitive and transparent bidding process to select counterparties rather than 
to follow  a pre -selection process. However, as part of this competitive bidding process, due 
diligence and other assessments will take place to ensure the counterparties have adequate 
financial, management, reporting and other capacities that are well established and functioning. 
The procurement of counterparties will proceed according to various finance policies, including 
controls and due diligence processes to ensure entities have the capacity to deliver.  
70. SPC confirms that it has conducted a formal assessment of its procurement, grants, risk 
and assets for the project, complemented by an informal review of the status of FSM according 
to the Financial Action Task Force. Risk factors and mitigation measures utilized are based on 
SPC’s Procurement Capacity Assessment  for Grant Recipients or Sub -delegates for FSM. 
71. SPC’s assessment ascertained that FSM has not been identified by the Financial Action 
Task Force  as having strategic deficiencies regarding anti -money laundering (AML) or 
countering the financing of terrorism  (CFT), and has a low risk for money laundering, terrorist 
financing, or smuggling. Acceptable international principles are being followed, and formal 
guidelines and procedures exist to identify, monitor and address conflicts of interest.  
72. Procedures are n on-discriminatory, provide transparency, equitability, and public 
access to relevant information. There are anti -fraud and corruption policies in place, with 
finances subject to periodic reviews, inspections and audits. Rules and regulations include fair 
and impartial mechanisms to address protests and policy violations, and there are formal 
guidelines and procedures to identify, monitor and address conflicts of interest. Ultimately, 
SPC’s assessment concluded that the severity for risks and vulnerabilities  in the project was 
‘low’.  
73. SPC advises that it has an AML/CFT policy that prevents the funding of prohibited 
activities through any of SPC's projects and initiatives, namely the  “Finance Policy: Anti -Money 
Laundering and Counter ing the Financing of Terror ism”  (effective July 2020). Moreover, SPC’s 
procurement team maintains a full set of documentation for every procurement process 
including purchase orders, contracts, solicitation documents, records of evaluations and 
communications with participants. This is consistent with SPC’s “ Finance Policies: Procurement 
Policy ” (effective July 2020), which includes assessment and mitigation of risks in all 
procurement processes.  
74. Due diligence requirements for vendors are established in the “ AML/CTF Policy, 
Procurem ent Policy ” and “Grants and Sub- delegations Policy ”. SPC undertakes due diligence 
when engaging any entity in activities to ensure the identity of the entity, enabling SPC to assess 
and evaluate risks accordingly. This is further supported by the “ Fraud an d Corruption Policy ” 
as outlined in SPC's “ Manual of Staff Policies ”. 
75. SPC demonstrates its commitment to excluding access to funding for entities that engage 
in certain activities, by exercising general conditions for contracts and grants that permit 
termi nation of such agreements with immediate effect where the other entity is involved in:  
(a) fraud or corruption;  
(b) money -laundering;  
(c) terrorism financing;  
(d) activities non -compliant with child protection;  
(e) activities undermining human rights; and/or  
(f) sexual harassment , sexual abuse or sexual exploitation. 
76. SPC confirms that it does not have any intention to distribute any items of value directly 
to any beneficiaries. The grants disbursed by the RCGF will be exclusively for goods and services 
provided directly by l ocal authorities, facilitating agents or contractors, as per the provisions of


---
*Page 96*
---

 
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 01 
Page 96 
 
 
their Operations Manual . This will proceed in accordance with the SPC policies that will be 
followed throughout to prevent prohibited practices, including  the: 
(a) Finance Regulations (20 18);  
(b) Finance Policy: Procurement (2020);  
(c) Finance Policy: Grants and Sub- delegations (2020); and  
(d) Finance Policy: Anti -Money Laundering and Counter ing the Financing of Terrorism g 
(2020).  
77. SPC advises that the disbursement of funds to successful grantees, as well as payments 
to Facilitating Agents and any other entities, will proceed according to the abovementioned 
policies. These policies ensure considerable internal controls and due diligence so that funds are 
properly disbursed and managed, and that funds are not used for prohibited or illicit activities. 
All entities receiving funds under the project will be compelled to comply with these policies.  
78. SPC has established a grievance and redress mechanism to ensure that complaints are 
addressed. Concerns about  conflicts of interest would be received by SPC’s Legal Team who will 
direct them to the Social  and Environmental Responsibility Helpdesk to initiate a conflict 
resolution process and develop an action plan or appropriate redress mechanism. If this is not 
effective, other processes may be followed according to SPC’s “Social and Environmental 
Responsibility Policy” (2020). The EDA programme will also have a dedicated grievance redress 
mechanism.  
79. Allegations of breaches of contract, violation of AML/CFT policies or involvement in 
illicit or unauthorized activities would be a breach of the Code of Conduct and will be investigated under SPC’s Staff Regulations and Manual of Staff Policies. Outcomes m ay result in 
summary dismissal, other action being taken against staff, or termination of contracts with 
cause for grantees, with other actions being taken as required.  
80. Recommended risk rating : The Office of Risk Management and Compliance 
(ORMC)/Compliance  Team has conducted a review of the project in accordance with relevant 
GCF Board approved policies and does not find any material issue or deviation with respect to compliance issues. Based on available information for this funding proposal, the 
ORMC/Comp liance Team have determined a risk rating of ‘medium’ and has no objection to this 
request proceeding to the next steps at this time.  
81. ORMC/Compliance would like to remind SPC, as the AE, of its continuing obligations and 
responsibilities with regard to monitoring and reporting any risks for money laundering, financing  of terrorism , or prohibited practices among the intended counterparties, executing 
entities, beneficiaries, persons involved, or any of the proposed activities.  
4.3.5.  GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)  
82. In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in 
terms of results area and single proposal is not material.  
4.3.6.  Recommendation  
83. It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision .

4.4 Fiduciary  
84. Pacific Community (SPC) will be the accredited entity and the executing entity for the 
project through a dedicated EDA Programme Coordination Unit (ECU), hosted within the SPC 
regional office in FSM.  
85. SPC’s Climate Change and Environmental Sustainability Division will receive funds from 
the G CF. These funds will in turn flow to the ECU as the e xecuting entity (EE) for the EDA 
programme. The EE in its capacity, and based on the grant applications received , will nter  into 
grants agreements with the LAs following the procedures on disbursement.  
86. SPC will be responsible for financial monitoring and for the financial execution of GCF 
funds according to SPC rules and regulations. As the a ccredited entity, SPC’s CFU within the 
Climate Change and Environmental Sustainability Division will be responsible for the overall 
project management.  
87. Results and outcomes from p rogramme activities will be monitored in accordance with 
SPC’s Planning, Evaluation, Accountability, Reflection and Learning (PEARL) policy. SPC policies 
on financial management including “Finance Regulations”, “Anti- money Laundering and 
Countering the Financing of T errorism”, “Procurement” and “Grants and S ub-delegation” as well 
as its “Staff Regulations and Manual of Staff Policies” that govern human resources and other 
relevant processes and procedures will be followed in the implementation of this project.  
4.5 Results monitoring and reporting  
88. As an adaptation programme, the intervention expects to benefit about 54,000 direct 
and 9 2,000 indirect beneficiaries, of which women comprise 49  per cent  of both direct and 
indirect beneficiaries across the three targeted sectors of food security, water security and 
disaster risk reduction.  
89. The programme aims at supporting  different types of adaptation activities which are 
expected to generate impact across the four adaptation result areas. This is clearly 
demonstrated in the theory of change diagram as well as in the annexed t able 4, which, as per 
guidance provided by the Secretariat, details the specific types of interventions supported by 
the programme as well as how they are foreseen to achieve measurable adaptation benefits.  
90. The revised logical framework integrates most of the comments and suggestions 
provided by the S ecretariat and it is assessed to have applied results measurements against the 
GCF Results Management Framework (RMF) and Performance Management Framework (PMF) 
sufficiently for quality at entry considerations. More robust methods of verification ( MoVs ) for a 
few outcome and programme  performance indicators have been  provided as per guidance 
shared with the AE.  
91. The implementation timetable in a nnex 5 is not aligned with the Programme Calendar 
under section 11 of the term sheet , while t he monitoring and eva luation plan in annex 11 does 
not include the full list of the logical framework indicators as requested by the Secretariat. Both 
annexes shall be updated at the FAA negotiation stage.  
4.6 Legal assessment

92. The accreditation master agreement (AMA) was signed with the a ccredited entity on 08 
November 2019, and it became effective on 10 April 2020.  
93. The accredited entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has 
obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and autho rity to implement the 
programme.  
94. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the f unding proposal to the Board : 
(a) The accredited entity has obtained all its internal approvals ; and  
(b) GCF  has received a certificate or legal opinion from the a ccredited entity in a form and 
substance satisfactory to GCF  confirming that all final internal approvals by the 
accredited entity have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to 
implement the programme.  
4.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)  
95. The proposed programme will be implemented in the Federated States of Micronesia. 
GCF has signed a bilateral agreement on privileges and immunities with the Federated States of 
Micronesia on 27 April 2016 and the agreement entered into force upon signature.  
96. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made 
subject to the following conditions:  
(a) Submission by the a ccredited entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form 
and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secr etariat, within 120 days after Board approval, 
confirming that the a ccredited entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by 
it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;  
(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement i n a form and substance satisfactory to the 
GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the 
accredited entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has 
obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and  
(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.
