Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment  of 
FP169 
Proposal name:  Climate change adaptation solutions for L ocal Authorities 
in the Federated States of Micronesia  
Accredited entity:  Pacific Community (SPC)  
Country/(ies):  Federated States of Micronesia  
Programme/programme size:  Small  
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 Impact potential                Scale:  High  
1. The Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) is an  archipelago located in the North Pacific 
Ocean. There are four States (Chuuk , Kosrae,  Pohnpei and Yap), which are widely dispersed in 
an east -west orientation. The four States are comprised of 607 islands, although habitation is 
found in only 74 of these islands. The eastern -most State (Kosrae) is located at a distance of 
2,700 kilomet res from the western -most State (Yap). The estimated population of FSM was 
113,815 in 2019. The per capita gross domestic product (GDP) was USD 3,400. It is found that 
approx imately 10 per cent of the population across FSM falls below the food poverty line, while 
41 per cent of the population falls below the total basic needs poverty line. The majority of the 
population is engaged in the subsisten ce production system involving  fisheries, agriculture and 
livestock rearing.    
2. The climatology of FSM exhibits strong seasonality  and inter -annual variabi lity. While El 
Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) has a strong influence on overall climate in  the nation, the 
total inter -annual rain fall is significantly affected by the two phases of ENSO, namely El Ni ño 
and La Ni ña. There are two seasons w ith the rainy season occurring from M ay to October. The 
general climatology is also influenced by the presence of the Indian Ocean Dipole and Inter -
tropical Convergence Zone. While the average monthly temperature hardly change s throughout 
the year, the monthly average rainfall amount exhibit s significant changes. The mean annual 
rainfall is around 4,826 mm. In general, the ea stern State s are much drier than the w estern 
States, where high intensity rainfall episodes are common. In sharp contrast, the eastern States 
are more affected due to higher levels of wave interactions along the shorelines, owing to much 
greater influence of sea surface temperatur e (SST) and SST anomalies.  
3. The climatological data density in FSM is commendable. The analysis of historical 
observational data exhibits nominal inter -decadal as well as inter -seasonal temperature change 
since 1950 across the four States. The decadal rate of change in mean temperature is in the 
order of 0.0 to 0.15˚C across seasonal and spatial scales. A n exception is found in the western 
State of Yap  during the wet season , when a gradual decrease (i.e. withdrawal) of annual mean as 
well as seasonal rainfall amount is found, with a considerably high er rate of withdrawal of 
rainfall during the dry season. Based on evidences and thorough analyses of available data, it is 
claimed that the combination of warming and withdrawal of dry season as well as ann ual 
rainfall has resulted in greater loss of moisture from the top soils leading to higher evapotranspiration and droughts. The intensity of drought is further compounded by the La 
Ni
ña phase of ENSO. Current ly scien tific opinion foresees that ENSO- related  influences on 
seasonality will worsen in a warm ing world, wi th a much greater influence on adverse climate -


---
*Page 100*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 100 
 
 
induced hazards and disasters. Climate change- influenced drought is therefore a great concern 
for FSM.  
4. The rainfall variability is found to be great er in the western State of Yap and its islets 
compared to eastern States. The western State is experiencing an increase in extreme rainfall 
leading to greater susceptibility of floods. Moreover, higher intensity rainfall events tend to 
erode top -soils and increased sedimentation in the downstream areas of the catchment, which 
chokes the available ponds and reduces water availability for irrigation purposes and for 
livestock rearing.  
5. However, a much -pronounced flooding effect is found in the eastern States d ue to 
increasing wave interactions that are caused by a combination of SST and sea level rise  (SLR ). 
Between 1993 and 2010, FSM has experienced a disproportionate amount of  SLR , ranging from 
8 to 12 mm per year, compared to other areas in the Western Pacif ic. Brunn’s equation of 1962 
suggests that increased sensitivity of cyclonic speed with increasing temperature translate s into 
an asymptotic increase in storm surge height with increasing SST. As a result of all these, there 
has been higher intensity of cyclonic events and associated coastal floodings across FSM. There 
is firm proof that the northern Pacific Ocean region is experiencing high SST and anecdotal 
evidence suggest s that there have been increased levels of inundation, particularly in the 
eastern islets.   
6. The compounding effects of rising SST and increasing sea level have resulted in much 
greater vulnerability of the coastal zone s due to increased flooding and inundation. 
Infrastructure including road networks, standing crops and water supply syste ms are heavily 
impacted. Transportation and communication between islands become increasingly difficult, 
while transportation of goods and services are severely constrained during such events. The 
destruction of road networks prevent s transfer of patients to health -care facilities. Saline 
inundation is often found to affect aquifer system s, making it difficult to collect non -saline 
potable water. Loss of income from fishing, cropping and livestock management increases food 
insecurity, especially among food impoverished households.  
7. Future climate change projection has used models that are validated with observational 
data. Even considering results based on a  low climate change scenario ( applying the 
Representative Climate Pathway 4.5 scenario, as defined by the I nter -governmental Panel on 
Climate Change), the frequency analyses of very high intensity hazardous events , such as 
droughts, floods and storm surge s, exhibit significant increase while extreme events will 
become increasingly frequent with time. The projection results clearly highlight that under 
climate change, even during a 2011 –2040 time frame, adverse impacts will be much aggravated. 
Due to an increase in rainfall, floods will occur more fr equently . Despite a general increase in 
projected rainfall, the seasonal withdrawal of rainfall in the dry seasons will aggravate seasonal 
drought. The continued rise in sea levels and ENSO -induced effects will have much aggravated 
impacts in relation to h igh intensity cyclonic storm surge. Such impacts  warrant  a portfolio of 
location -specific projects and adaptive activities primarily in the inhabited islets, if not in all the 
islands and atolls.  
8. The project does not present a portfolio of location -specific projects. Rather it takes 
advantage of the e nhanced direct access (EDA) option to mobilize a significant amount under a 
proposed EDA Grant Facility for the creation of a portfolio of locally relevant adaptation projects to be administered by local author ities (LA s). However, the project took note of a barrier 
analysis and simultaneously focused on institutional capacity -building so that State 
governments and municipalities can enhance their capacities to design and deliver on -the-
ground adaptation activit ies. 
9. The project has two components. Component 1 deals with empowerment of LAs to 
deliver climate change adaptation services to their population, while Component 2 focuses on 
priority project implementation by creating an EDA facility for strengthening local co mmunity


---
*Page 101*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 101 
 
 
resilience. The m ajority of the proposed fund (i.e. USD 12 million out of a total of USD 19.71 
million) is earmarked for the proposed  EDA grant facility. The project , including a host of EDA 
grant sub -projects , will be implemented within seven year s after  inception at a total estimated 
cost of about USD 19.71 million.    
10. Coverage of the project in terms of reaching direct beneficiaries is commendable. About 
47 per cent of the FSM population (53,639 people) will benefit directly.  Indirect benefits are 
likely to reach up to 80 per cent of the population (91,052 people) across the four States of FSM. 
Based on the initial screening and triangulation through stakeholder consultations, the 
vulnerable islands are identified. However, th e list of vulnerable islands thus created will not be 
the only criterion for the mobilization of EDA grants. The capacity of LAs will also be considered 
in awarding EDA grants. E fforts will be made to build cap acity of the weak but highly vulnerable 
munici palities so that they will also have  a fair opportunity to participate in the grant 
application processes and utilize the EDA grant support.  
11. The available information clearly suggests that the impact potential of the proposed 
project is high.   
1.2 Paradigm sh ift potential              Scale:  High  
12. The project identifies t he following two components to deliver the intended benefits: (a) 
Component 1: Local Authorities (L As) are empowered to deliver climate change adaptation 
services to their population; and (b) Component 2: priority project implementation – EDA 
facility to be created for strengthening local community resilience. While the focus of 
Component 1 is to strengthen local- level capacities , including that of local institutions,  through 
capacity -building , Component 2 is aimed at disbursement of financial resources to LAs with 
capacity and the need to address local -level climate change adaptation challenges. The project 
was conceived under the EDA window and therefore its central activities will be supported 
through deliver y of EDA grant s to LAs.  
13. The LAs seeking EDA grant s are primarily at municipal level and will be selected on the 
basis of willingness to participate in the EDA grant programme. Higher preference will be g iven 
to LAs that have proven capacity to deliver local- level activities and to those that exhibit high 
fiduciary standards in disbursing resources. Vulnerability to climate change at local level will not be the primary  criteria for selecting likely grant -recipient LAs. However, clear preference 
will be given to those LAs that can exhibit capacity to deliver and willingness to develop a 
climate change adaptation project according to specific criteria and following GCF investment 
criteria. It may be apprehend ed that LAs with relatively weaker capacity and higher climate 
vulnerability could fall behind in accessing adaptation finance under such selection approaches. 
To address this, the project management unit will offer institutional and technical support to 
LAs with proven high climate change vulnerabilities in order to enhance  their capacit ies and 
enable them to deliver adaptation services to the most vulnerable islets and people.  
14. Implementin g a combination of the above two components will not only create de mand -
driven access to adaptation finance, bu t will also build the capacity of weak but climate -
vulnerable  LAs to have greater access to much needed finance to administer local level 
adaptation. The approach is innovative and will lead to a paradigm shift away from constrained 
institutional and technical capacities to increased knowledge and technical ability . This will 
enable local communities  to translate their needs into viable climate adaptation sub -projects 
based on local -level vulnerabilities. The stre ngthening of institutions’ and communities’ 
capacity to discuss, document and plan for climate change adaptation will pave the way to a 
systematic approach to adaptation actions at local levels, representing a shift from an ad hoc 
approach to sustainable d elivery of community -based adaptation process es.


---
*Page 102*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 102 
 
 
15. The EDA grant sub -project delivery is innovative at local level, because it draws on the 
good practices of GCF and aims to customize the m to suit local realities. There are parallels 
between the proposed grant endorsing body and the GCF Board ; the proposed programme 
management unit and the GCF Secretariat ; and the establishment of a non -biased body, similar  
to the i ndependent Technical Advisory Panel ( TAP)  to apply the custom ized criteria f or EDA 
sub-projects  to assist with judicious selection based on technical merit . These approaches are 
innovative at the national level, providing ample opportunities to learn from and replicate in 
other countries.  
16. In addition to the replica tion potential of sub -project delivery processes, the proposed 
combination  of institutional capacity -building and subsequent assistance with applications for 
much -needed dedicated funds also has replication potential in other small island developing 
State s (SIDS). The demand -driven structure for  accessing financial support, on the basis of a 
clear understanding of local -level vulnerabilities and clear identification of adaptation 
measures, can be replicated in many countries. It is expected that all program me outputs and 
results will be collated and archived in centralized data portals, which will be a source of 
updated information on climate change vulnerability and adaptation involving multiple islets 
and atolls located a cross the four Federated States of Micronesia. The information data base is 
expected to be open source to guide scaling up of adaptation activities in municipalities that will 
remain outside the EDA grants programme.  
17. Under Component 2 , dedicated activity 2.3 is expected to focus on finalizing a resource 
mobilization strategy to secure funding sources to recapitalize the EDA f acility and prepare 
handover of its management. It is also anticipated that additional finding from both public and 
private bodies will b e secured to encourag e the extension of the EDA f acility to a second phase 
in which a number of LAs could be engaged .  
18. It is perceived that EDA grant -seeking efforts will be based on local -level understanding 
of three aspects: disaster risk reduction, wate r security and food security. The local -level 
responses will be based on these aspects, taking into consideration understanding and 
knowledge o f issues such as rainwater catchment management and storage of water to address 
drought ; cyclone proofing through  mangrove enhancement and the creation of breakwaters ; 
ecosystem services and management of endemic species;  marine protected areas ; and 
management of small- scale livestock and fisheries, etc. Knowledge o f the  causes of 
vulnerabilities and of targeted resp onses is key to identif ying specific response measures, which 
will be central i n justifying grant for sub-projects. The project, therefore, is focused on 
knowledge, while leaving ample opportunity for learning.  
19. Component 1 of the project has been specifica lly designed to create pathways to 
facilitate the generation and subsequent dissemination of knowledge through the learning 
exchanges. There are opportunities for local actors , including communit ies and institutional 
actors , to engage in trainings. Lessons  learned in the design and implementation of the capacity -
building activities as well as trainings,  is likely to help inform the development of future EDA 
and local -level capacity -building projects. The cross- learning opportunities are likely to help the 
development of EDA grant sub -projects in other atolls and islets through replication of 
processes and methodologies. The targeted capacity enhancement support from the project 
delivery side to relatively weak LAs will have lasting impacts in conceiving loca l adaptive 
actions and delivering responses upon receipt of EDA grants. The proposed data hub, web 
portals and periodically organized workshops will further enable cross -learning involving 
actors representing various municipalities and state governments across FSM.  
20. Based on the effective implementation of small -scale, locally relevant and community -
centric adaptation projects, the budgetary allocation for such activities will be incentivised and 
simultaneously it is expected to cataly se local and national policy change in view of institutional 
integration of adaptation. The processes of highlighting local adaptive actions and needs for


---
*Page 103*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 103 
 
 
budgetary allocations will feed into national policies, eventually contributing to the creation of 
an enabling environment.  
21. Under Component 1, the capacity of LAs will be built towards a greater understanding o f 
climate change impacts to develop and implement their local -level priority adaptive activities. 
The integration of local communities in to the planning process and prioritiz ation of adaptive 
actions  will also contribute to an enabling environment by incent ivising community ownership, 
which will in turn leverage local contributions and supervision of operation and maintenance 
(O&M) activities – these aspects being vital for long -term sust ainability  of the adaptive actions.  
22. Analyses on local (participatory) vulnerability assessments and updated analyses of 
adaptation actions including their local -level effectiveness , are expected to enable local 
institutions to incrementally extend adaptive actions.  This knowledge will enable institutions to 
serve the populat ion under their jurisdiction. The service delivery frameworks envisaged within 
the project purview are likely to support bottom -up advocacy at both state and national levels, 
thereby further strengthening local enabling capacities.     
23. The overall objectiv e of the project is to build local adaptive capacities by strengthening 
stakeholders’ cap acities , plans and adaptive infrastructure involving green, grey and blue small -
scale infrastructure. Most of these deliverables are locally suited, tested for their effectiveness, 
and generally are no regret options. As a result, the y are likely to contribute to incrementally 
build ing adaptive capacities that are easy to replicate at micro -scale . If finances are streamlined, 
and O&M procedures are not complicated they will  therefore be sustainable in the long run. The 
simplicity of approaches and people -centric execution of measures are expected to enable the 
local institutions to deliver climate -resilient pathways for the island nation.  
24. In view of the above discussions , the i ndependent TAP is of the opinion that the 
paradigm shift potential of the proposed project is high.   
1.3 Sustainable development potential             Scale:  High  
25. The project relies heavily on EDA grant sub -projects, which are awaiting development. It 
is therefore premature to decisively forecast the potential contribution of the project to single 
Sustainable Development Goals (SDG s). Since the project has gone thro ugh an initial scoping of 
potential vulnerable municipalities and their climate change -related adaptation needs, there 
exists a rough understanding regarding potential adaptive capacity -building and interventions. 
This understanding on adaptation responses  may be organized under three broad categories: 
(1) disaster risk reduction (DRR); (2) water security including provision for drinking water; and 
(3) food security. There will be adaptive responses involving mangrove enrichment, natural 
resources managemen t involving terrestrial and marine environments, and biodiversity 
conservation. Based on these responses, it may be inferred that the proposed project will 
contribute to a number of SDGs, the most notable being : (a) SDG 1: No poverty; ( b) SDG 2: Zero 
hunge r; (c) SDG 3: Good health and well -being; (d) SDG 6: Clean water and sanitation ; and ( e) 
SDG 14: Life below water. In addition, contributions to two other SDGs are most likely in terms 
of SDG 5: Gender equality ; and SDG 13: Enhanced climate actions.  
1.3.1.  Enviro nmental co -benefits  
26. Blue and green infrastructure for achieving disaster risk reduction by means of 
establishing marine safe havens through breakwaters and mangrove protective belt s, 
respectively, have potentials for providing significant environmental co -benefits  in terms of 
biodiversity refuge and conservation. Slope protection in undulated terrains will tend to reduce soil erosion, thereby enhancing soil conservation. Potential infiltration of water in irrigated 
lands will tend to strengthen the recharge  potential of aquifers. Watershed management and 
restoration activities are likely to improve water quality and reduce pollution load in the marine


---
*Page 104*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 104 
 
 
environment. Micro -scale air quality improvement may be envisaged from the use of solar 
water pumps. On a similar note, miniscule mitigation benefits (not estimated as such) may be 
accrued through agroforestry activities, pumping water using solar pumps instead of using 
fossil fuel -based technologies and by sequestering carbon in the mangroves.  
1.3.2.  Economic co -ben efits   
27. Reducing the loss and damage burden s that are arising from climate change -induced 
extreme weather events and strengthening livelihoods will offer the most significant economic 
co-benefit. The protection infrastructure will help avoid direct damages that occur during high 
intensity adverse weather events. The small- scale ponds will tend to ameliorate drought 
impacts by replenishing top soil moisture during the critical growth stage of crops, th us help ing 
to maintain crop productivity. The investments in micro -scale community roads will help 
maintain physical communication during cyclonic storms and floods, thereby contributin g to 
smooth mobilization of goods and services. The micro -scale livestock and fisheries activities will 
help to creat e employment  through a robust production system, while the assistance in 
establishing a value chain f or local products will help producers to gain economically from their  
activities.  
1.3.3.  Social co-benefits   
28. Reducing the risks that emanate from invigorated weather events i s already beneficial to 
communit ies in any country. The structures to be put in place will reduce disaster risks. The 
restoration of road networks will help speedy mobilization of goods and services in the 
aftermath of disastrous events, allowing patients to be taken to health care facilities and 
restor ing social cohesion following disasters. The increased income from productive activities 
will have positive social outcomes in terms of enhanced employment and reduction of poverty.  
29. The water tanks will direc tly serve potable water, which will have significant health care 
benefits. The livestock and fisheries sub -programme, in conjunction with enhanced availability 
of marine fisheries due to mangrove conservation and refuge protection through breakwater 
creati on will have significant positive outcomes in terms of availability of protein. Meanwhile, 
the micro -scale water retention ponds to reduce drought effects will tend to contribute directly 
to attain greater food security, which will have social co -benefit p otential.  
1.3.4.  Gender -related co -benefits  
30. In any society where community -based DRR efforts are judicously considered, it is often 
observed that women almost automatically benefit from such activities. Such benefits are 
generally optimized when women’s voice are duly included in project design. The  gender action 
plan of the project indicates that women’s particular concerns  are embraced and provisions for 
advancing the cause of women  are included . The project envisages that sub -grants will consider 
gender -focused activities, while giving priority to  voices in favour of gender equity during sub -
project design. Women and men will benefit equally fr om many of the outcomes highlighted 
above, especially where social co -benefits are significant. Integration of women in local -level 
production system s and va lue chains , and inclusion of women in various committees and 
decision -making bodies will help advance gender concerns.  
31. Based on the various attributes that bring different types of co -benefits, the independent 
TAP finds the sustainable development potenti al of the project is high.  
1.4 Needs of the recipient               Scale:  High


---
*Page 105*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 105 
 
 
32. As discussed under the section on impact potential, FSM is a country vulnerable to 
climate change. A combination of highly variable rainfall, the influence of ENSO, the ever -
increasing surface , as well as sea surface , temperature and rising amplitude of extreme tides 
that are compounded by rising sea levels , have resulted in m ore intense incidents of high 
intensity extreme weather events such as cyclonic storm surge s, droughts and floods. The low -
lying islets and atoll system and the high density of inhabitants living within met res of the sea 
make the system highly vulnerable to climate change -induced hazards. FSM’s poor state of 
readiness to deal with climatic hazard s is also noteworthy. In terms of climate change 
preparedness FSM is currently ranked 78th  least ready country the Notre Dame Global 
Adaptation Initiative identifies it as the fourth most vulnerable country to climate change. These 
rankings demonstrate tha t the country is indeed highly vulnerable and therefore the need for an 
early response is critically important.  
33. Even when  island economies are integrated with the global economy, such integration 
often comes at a high cost. The vast majority of the populat ion in FSM is reliant on 
underdeveloped as well as subsisten ce agriculture, livestock and fisheries value systems. 
Consequently, 41 per cent of the population l ives below the poverty line, where an average of 
USD 1.84 per adult per day is needed to meet es sential calorific requirement s.1 The subsisten ce 
production system results in 10 per cent of the population below the food poverty line in the 
FSM. The economy of FSM i s not self -reliant  and is highly dependent on Compact of Free 
Association ( COFA ) financi ng. The COFA currently funds 80 per cent of the State s’ budgets and 
over 90 per cent of its funding is allocated for health and education. The overall financial 
outlook against  the backdrop of uncertainties regarding COFA financing appears bleak, 
especially after 2023 when the current COFA financing phase will  expire. This indicates that 
FSM is in dire need of international support to finance its much -needed adaptation -related 
activities in the wake of climate change -induced vulnerabilities.  
34. The current technical and financial capacities of FSM at local, State and national levels 
are severely constrained in view of growing vulnerabilities to climate change. A study  financed  
by USAID highlights that the estimated costs for operationalizing cli mate change policies and 
actions at State and local levels far outweigh the budgetary provisions of relevant organizations 
operating at various tiers. F inancial support from development partners is found to be 
inadequate compared to the overall financing g ap. Moreover, over 60 per cent of the funds are 
uncoordinatedly mobilized by national decision -makers, which results in widening the  financing 
gap for climate adaptation. It is found that the financial requirement for advancing adaptation in 
FSM is in the order of USD 1.4 billion, which exceeds FSM’s national gross domestic product 
(GDP) five -fold. Moreover, there is a significant capacity gap in terms of human resources to 
understand and assess climate change -related vulnerabilities and to devis e plans at different 
governance tiers to address such vulnerabilities. All these have left a significant capacity void as 
regards mak ing any headway towards climate resilience in FSM.  
35. The project proposal is supported by a barrier analysis, which highlights a number  of 
capacity barriers including institutional barriers. It is found that the FSM State and municipal 
authorities generally do not have institutional capacities to respond to the adverse effects of 
climate change. In fact, the generic inability to analyse v ulnerabilities and respond to the m, 
compounded by lack of coordination, information  and adequate human resources, severely 
constrain delivery of adaptation services through institutional approaches. The proposal claims 
that the level of climate -related und erstanding among decision -makers is generally high. Yet, 
due to the inability of institutions to deliver adaptatio n services they remain  inaccessible to the 
population . Component 1 of the proposed project aims at fostering the transfer, receipt and 
integra tion of knowledge across LAs  thus recognising the need to strengthen institutions which 
have a mandate at the national and State level s to deliver adaptation -related services.    
 
1 Based on 2013 –2014 estimation.


---
*Page 106*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 106 
 
 
36. Adaptation responses at community level work better if incremental fit -for-purpose 
steps are designed and gradually delivered by involving the community -level stakeholders. 
However, if there is a capacity constrain t at community level, generally adaptation at the 
grassroots become s ineffective. The project rightly acknowledges the capacity gaps prevailing at 
community level and focuses on building capacities to implement local -level adaptations in 
cooperation with local institutions. Not only will the financial needs be addressed, but the 
project will also enhanc e knowledge and tech nical know -how through trainings, includ ing for 
women a nd institutional actors. Since the sub-grant selection processes will follow GCF -style 
investment criteria w ith an emphasis on training, learning opportunities and capacity -building, 
it is expected that the sub-grant proposals  will highlight these aspects, recognising the 
importance of community capacity needs. It is anticipated that such capacity -building efforts at 
community levels will revolve around DRR, water provisioning and water security and also 
sustainable agriculture and food security.  
37. Although the project focuses on EDA grant support to LAs and communities, it exhibits 
high sensitivity to various needs of t he recipients at both institutional and community levels 
across the four States of FSM. The i ndependent TAP therefore rates the needs of the recipients 
as high.  
1.5 Country ownership               Scale:  High  
38. The project , along with its deliverables , is in full conformity with climate change policy 
(2013), and the specific objectives are in alignment with the nationwide integrated disaster risk 
management and climate change policy (2013) of FSM. Two of the strategic objectives of the 
latter policy should be h ighlighted : 1) strategic objective #2 aims to “Promote, facilitate and 
develop training programs focused on disaster risk management and climate change for 
scientific, technical, managerial personnel and policy makers ” and strategic objective #7 aims t o 
“Reduce and manage the risks associated with more frequent, severe and unpredictable 
extreme weather events” . The climate change adaptation objectives are in alignment with the 
second national communication of FSM to the United Nations Framework Conventio n on 
Climate Change, where emphasis is placed on achieving food and water security and protection 
against coastal flooding , both of which are aggravated by climate change. The approach of 
mobilizing institutional stakeholders and communities also finds con formity with the 
community -based adaptation approach of both the climate change policy and second national 
communication of FSM.  
39. The nationally determined contribution to achieve objectives of the Paris Protocol 
highlights priority adaptation actions that are in line with the project deliverables. The document emphasizes the urgency o f enhanc ing local capacity to plan, design , implement, 
manage and operate adaptation projects, which are at the core of the proposed project.  The FSM 
Climate Change Act (2014) provides for the regulatory functions and directives on climate 
change in FSM, whereas the project finds great overlaps in approach and delivery.  
40. In addition to having national -level policy alignment, the project is also exhibiting good 
alignment with Sta te-level Joint State Action Plans for disaster risk management and climate 
change that refer to Chuuk, Kosrae, Pohnpei and Yap States. These State- level action plans are 
focused on regional differences and needs in terms of DRR, f ood and water security, an d take 
into consideration the  spatial dynamics of vulnerability to climate change -induced hazards and 
disasters.  
41. The municipalities of FSM have developed local early action plans (LEAPs) as part of the 
International Climate Initiative where causes of spec ific vulnerabilities and adaptation needs 
are highlighted. It is expected that the EDA grant sub -projects will find inputs from respective 
LEAPs, verify them through stakeholder engagement and be integrated into EDA sub -projects.


---
*Page 107*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 107 
 
 
This will ensure integrati on of existing policies while also devising and delivering adaptation in 
all tiers of governance in FSM.  
42. The FSM country programme submitted to GCF identifies the need for achieving food 
and water security, initiatives on DRR and resilient infrastructure a nd ecosystem strengthening. 
Therefore, it may be inferred that the project builds on identified priorities  that are already 
shared with G CF. The same country programme recognizes the difficulties in leveraging private 
sector financing for climate change ad aptation projects, while highlighting the general need for 
financing assistance to advance adaptation activities in FSM. The EDA Grant Facility has already 
been identified as one of the key mechanisms to mobilize finance for implementation of 
adaptation pr ojects. The involvement of LAs in the delivery of adaptation and for providing 
leadership for O&M in the continuous monitoring and mentorship of adaptive infrastructure has been part of the country programme, and is the central consideration of the proposed project.  
43. The Pacific Community (SPC)2 is a regional inter -governmental body operating in the 
Pacific region to foster scientific and technical cooperation for regional development. The SPC 
has been operational since 1947 and has strongly influenced the Pacific islands’ development. 
The SPC embraces 26 member countries and  advances sustainable development through the 
application of its knowledge base, science and technology in addressing cross -cutting issues 
such as DRR, climate change adaptation, food se curity, gender equality, management of non -
communicable diseases, human rights and employment of youth. The SPC is an accredited entity 
(AE) to GCF  and has already implemented a few small- scale adaptation projects by mobilizing 
finance received from international development partners. In the context of adaptation delivery, 
even beyond the regional scale in the Pacific, SPC has the right exposure and experiences to 
deliver adaptation at community levels involving national and State actors in FSM.  The SPC has 
a regional office based in Pohnpei, which has administered a number of adaptation projects in 
FSM including the G lobal Climate Change Alliance Plus project for  scaling up adaptation in the 
Pacific and small island developing States . 
44. The FSM Department of Finance and Administration  is the executing entity (EE) and also 
the national designated authority (NDA) for FSM. The NDA has received two readiness supports 
from GCF and successfully implemented the support projects. The NDA has been instrumental in  
designing a p revious  GCF approved project , the Pacific Islands Renewable Energy Investment 
Programme  (FP036 ). It is expected that the NDA will remain the focal point in provid ing 
guidance and decisions on EDA grant awards, while the four States will be fu lly involved and are 
expected to play key strategic roles in the programmatic implementation. It is expected that a 
national decision -making body consisting  of representatives from key stakeholders will endorse 
key milestones and lead key programme implementation including the selection of sub -grants. 
As already planned, resources will be mobilized through the NDA and LAs to foster the 
partnerships between the SPC and the channels mobilizing funds and to ensure ownership.  
45. The SPC has taken the lead to devi se the EDA project in cooperation with the NDA. The 
SPC mobilized strong support f rom  the NDA to engage stakeholders across FSM, including all 
four States , which hav e also engaged civil society organizations and women ’s groups. This has 
allowed the NDA and  SPC to combine  scientific knowledge and awareness of climate change 
adaptation with social adaptation priorities.  These factors played a significant part in the 
planning of the EDA grant sub -project together with the expected  institutional capacity -
building.  
46. In view of the above discussions, it appears to the independent TAP that country 
ownership of the project is high. 
 
2 Previously known as the South Pacific  Commission. It has retained its acronym.

*Page 108*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 108 
 
 
1.6 Efficien cy and effectiveness              Scale:  High  
47. The project will be implemented over  seven years at an estimated cost of USD 19.71 
million. The project proposes a total  of USD 16 .59 million  in the form of grants. Considering the 
nation’s status as s mall island developing State, the request for full concessionality is justified. 
The greater share of the finance for the project will be dedicated to provid ing grant support to 
LAs under a n EDA grant window, which accounts for about 71 per cent of the total budget. The 
co-financing amount is about USD 3.12 million, most of which is provided by the FSM 
Government. The co -financing ratio with respect to GCF financing is about 0.19 ∶1. 
48. As indicated in the earlier sections, there is a dearth of financing for climate ch ange 
adaptation in FSM. Neither national budgetary allocations nor private sector financing are 
available for building resilience in FSM. Since the majority of financing is earmarked for 
community -drive n, small -scale , and incrementally contributing no regret adaptation actions, 
there will not be any interest from the private sector to leverage adaptation financing. Therefore, the need t o mobiliz e GCF grant financing through the involvement of public sector 
institutions is critically important.  
49. The project presents budgetary allocation that identifies high value for money, as the 
estimated transaction cost in an island setting is comparable to projects financed by GCF in 
other countries where actual unit costs for variou s adaptation services do not require 
significant transportation and other costs. Since many of the activities will be accomplished by 
local communities and will be directed by LAs, the re is very little  requirement for engaging 
expatriate support and consultants compared to most GCF financed projects.  
50. The long distances between islands , at times unfavourable weather, logistics and high -
cost challenges of inter -island transportation in FSM make it particularly difficult to deliver 
goods and services . Any ana lysis of economic performance must take into consideration  the 
context of project delivery in FSM. Yet t he proposed financial structure of the  EDA grant  facility 
is deemed adequate and reasonable in order to achieve the p roject’s objectives while 
minimizin g transaction costs, in particular given the high complexity of targeting all 
municipalities in each of the States in order to include particularly vulnerable populations living 
in rural areas and outer  islands. It is found that the cost of delivery of sim ilar grant programme s 
in other Pacific islands per direct beneficiary is far  above the proposed cost of delivery per 
beneficiary under the proposed project (ranging from USD 436 to USD 8,415 as against USD 370 
for the proposed project).  
51. The incremental adaptation benefits present the basis for conducting the economic 
analysis of the project. The economic benefit -cost analysis consid ers a cash flow model over a 
20-year period as well as the accrual of DRR benefits from coastal infrastructure for a period of 
50 years. However, the analyses assume most probable adaptive responses under EDA grant sub-projects, on the basis of past interventions in various atolls and islets a s well as best 
practices from past projects. Since actual activities for the EDA grant window cannot be 
ascertained upfront, resorting to such analytical methodology is justified.  
52. The overall economic performance of the proposed project under the above 
assumptions appears effective and economically high ly desirable. The economic net present 
value (ENPV) appears to be not only positive, but the estimated ENPV is almost twice the amount to be spent in FSM. Moreover, the economic internal rate of return appears  to be  11 per 
cent, which indicates that the proj ect’s economic performance is likely to be attractive. The 
economic soundness looks even more attractive with the realization that about 30 per cent of the budgetary allocation is earmarked for non -investment budget costs for which no direct 
benefits may be accrued. However, the inputs for institutional and community -wide capacity -
building will eventually result in non -tangible benefits, which are  expected to contribute to 
long -term sustainability as well as replication of adaptation actions on the ground.


---
*Page 109*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 01 
Page 109 
 
 
53. The micro - and small -scale adaptive responses are yet to be defined in an  EDA  financing 
arrangement. However, the broad categories of responses against most known hazards are 
identified, and will form the basis for developing atoll - and islet -specific adaptations under EDA 
grant sub -projects. Since the responses will be evaluated against a set of criteria, as in the case 
of GCF investment criteria, it is anticipated that small -scale responses are most likely to be 
designed involving industry best pr actices. Moreover, the nature of small grants under the EDA 
grant facility does not allow for the choice of over -sophisticated technologies at exorbitant 
prices, but instead encourages the adoption of industry best practices.   
54. Based on the above findings, the i ndependent TAP is of the opinion that the effectiveness 
and efficiency of the project are  high.  
II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
55. The proposed EDA project is a good  example of this kind of  project and the independent 
TAP r ecommends the Board to approve the project.

Overview  
This Gender Analysis and Action Plan (GAAP) provides the overall context and framework for 
mainstreaming gender into the Green Climate Fund  (GCF) project “ Climate change adaptation solutions 
for local authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia. ” Accordingly , it has been developed in line with  
the GCF Gender Policy and the GCF Gender Mainstreaming Guide and includes the following sections:  
• Overview of the legal and policy framework related to gender in the Federated States of 
Micronesia ( FSM ) including a look at the Gender Stocktake Report for FSM  
• Analysis of population, household, education, and income data from the 2010 Census and 2013/2014 Household Income Survey  
• Description of income and employment in the agricultural sectors by gender from the FSM 
Agricultural Survey  
• Overview of v iolence against women issues in FSM  
• Overview of the SDG Voluntary National Review of national priorities and progress assessment for gender equality
1  
• Comparison of gender indicators for FSM and other Pacific Island Countries    
• Assessment of gender considerations for priority sector areas  
• Recommendations for sub -grant s mainstreaming gender  
• Description of sub- grant  implementation roles and responsibilities with regards to gender 
mainstreaming  
• Overall Gender Action Plan for the sub- grant  activities  
• Framework for gender mainstreaming in sub -grant s 
Methodology  
This GAAP was completed through a desk review of FSM’s legal and policy framework and publicly 
available gender data pertinent to this assessment. Consultations with women’s groups for each of the four 
States in FSM were additionally undertaken through November –December 2020 . The women’s 
groups /representatives  consulted were the following:  
1) Pohnpei Women’s Council  
2) Yap State Gender Support Officer representing Yap women’s groups  
3) Kosrae Women’s Council and  
4) Chuuk Women’s Council  
The consultation for the Pohnpei Women’s Council was completed in person; however, due to the ongoing 
COVID -19 pandemic, the remaining consultations were completed via virtual meetings led by the national 
consultant.  
The consultations focused on how gender vulnerabilities manifest within the three thematic areas covered 
by the proposed EDA programme (disaster risk reduction and coastal protection, food security and water 
security) and how the sub -grants cou ld be designed and implemented in a gender sensitive manner.  
Through the consultations, food security projects and assisting women farmers and gardeners were 
deemed as priority measures for the EDA programme to reduce gender vulnerabilities in FSM. Please see 
Annex I for the full summary of women’s groups consultations  completed .   
 
1 https://sustainabledevelopment.un.org/content/documents/26668VNR_2020_Micronesia_Report.pdf

Gender  Analysis and Assessment  
Legal and Policy Framework  
International and Bilateral treaties and agreements that FSM is party to and supports: 
• Article X of the Federal Programm e and Services Agreement between the Government of the 
Federated States of Micronesia and the Government of the United States where the US Agency for 
International Development (USAID) is responsible for providing disaster assistance and coordinating 
the US  Government’s response to declared disasters in the FSM.    
• Chapter 24 of Agenda 21 (United Nations Conference on Environment and Development, 1992).  
•  Convention on Biodiversity (1992).  
• Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action ( 1995, Fourth World Conference on Women).  
• the Johannesburg Plan of Implementation of the 2002 World Summit on Sustainable Development.  
• the 2000 Millennium Declaration.  
• the 2015 Paris Agreement of the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).  
• the Agenda 2030 for the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).  
• Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2018 – 2030.  
• Ratification of the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1993, UN Convention on the 
Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Agai nst Women (CEDAW) in 2004, and the UN Convention 
on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD) in 2016.    
• The FSM Government presented its combined Initial & First to Third CEDAW Report in 2018 and the 
CRC Report in 2020.   General observations and recommendations have been tabled to the FSM 
Government for consideration in follow up actions by the review panel of experts under CEDAW and CRC.  
• FSM Government participated in the UN Human Rights Council (HRC) and presented its report and progress in the imp lementation of human rights obligations and commitments during the 3
rd Universal 
Periodic Review (UPR) process in January 2021. A list of Recommendation had been tabled by the 
member governments of the HRC on matters pertaining to gender and social inclusi on issues including 
climate change and disaster risk management, food, and water.  
 
Examples of Recommendations include the following:  
• The submission to the UN of the initial report on the implementation of the Convention on the Rights 
of Persons with Disabilities.  
• Withdraw of reservations to the UN CEDAW.  
• Implement a national development plan to provide a strategic and coordinated framework for 
informing climate change mitigation efforts and building the country’s climate resilience.  
• Create a national development plan to mitigate the effects of climate change and increase funding 
and resources for emergency preparedness and response facilities as part of the ongoing 
strengthening of its overall climate resilience and mitigation efforts.  
• Ensure that the Nati onal Climate Change Policy considers efforts to alleviate the disproportionate 
impact of climate change on women and children.  
• Ensure that the National Policy on Climate Change considers the impact of climate change on 
children and women and the role they should have in the actions implemented in their favour.


---
*Page 116*
---

 
 
 
 
 
 
• Ensure that women, especially rural women, children, persons with disabilities and indigenous and 
local communities are meaningfully engaged in the development and implementation of climate change and disaster risk reduction frameworks.  
• Allocate sufficient resources to FSM Department of Environment, Climate Change and Emergency Management (DECEM).  
• Consider becoming a member of the International Labour Organization with the view to ratifying its 
core c onventions and bring its labour legislation in line with international standards.  
• Improve national data collection by completing a census by 2022 and ensure that disaggregated data 
supports national programming and decision- making in the field of climate c hange, domestic violence, 
gender -based violence, universal health coverage, universal access to sexual and reproductive 
health and rights, and empowerment of young people.

Based Violence and Gender Responsive Climate Justice.      
 
National  policies  and institutional arrangements  
According to the Pacific Women FSM gender situation analysis , the country is in the early stages of 
implementing a legislative and supporting policy framework to promote gender equality and women’s 
human rights, including co mmitments made under the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of 
Discrimination Against Women ( CEDAW) . It has a National Strategic Plan on Gender Development and 
Human Rights in place and a National Gender Policy . In 2019,  FSM states drafted gender -based violence 
action plans under the national Elimination of Violence Against Women and Girls policy (with support from 
the United Nations Population Fund) . The Pohnpei State Family Protection Act was passed in November 
2017  (although has yet to be enacted) , and Kosrae State Family Protection Act was passed in 2014. Chuuk 
and Yap States have yet to pass family protection legislation.2 
 
2 Pacific Women FSM Country Plan Summary; Available at: https://pacificwomen.org/key -pacific -women -resources/fsm -country -
plan- summary/ https://pacificwomen.org/key -pacific -women- resources/fsm -country -plan- summary/

The FSM Strategic Development Plan (2004– 2023) further highlights seven specific strategic goals for 
addressing gender and vulnerable groups as part of country level development:3 
• Strategic Goal 1:  Enhance and promote the cultural, economic, legal, political and social 
development of women and children throughout their life cycles  
• Strategic Goal 2:  Enhance the leadership c apacity and roles of women  
• Strategic Goal 3:  Mainstream gender issues into decision making, policies and strategic 
development plans  
• Strategic Goal 4:  Maximize women’s contribution to and participation in democratic and 
development processes by creating op portunities for women’s active involvement.  
• Strategic Goal 5:  Strengthen the institutional capacity of the women’s programs in FSM  
• Strategic Goal 6:  Strengthen the institutional capacity, effectiveness and impact of youth 
organizations  
• Strategic Goal 7:  Strengthen youth development through social, economic and political 
participation  
The National Gender Policy, endorsed in 2018, has six strategic goal s, linked with other gender equality 
commitments including the S ustainable Development Programme, CEDAW, Pacific 
Leaders  Gender  Equality Declaration  and the SDGs :  
• Goal 1: Better representation of women in decision- making  
• Goal 2: Elimination of gender -based violence  
• Goal 3: Equitable education outcomes  
• Goal 4: Address barriers facing women in the workforce  
• Goal 5: Women have better health care and improved choices over their fertility  
• Goal 6: FSM and State Governments mainstream gender  
The Policy recognizes climate change and disaster risk reduction (DRR) as priority cross -cutting issues , 
noting that adaptation can avoid exacerbating existing gender  inequalities and vulnerabilities by planning 
for population and cultural impacts. The short -term prioriti es include gender -based violence (with the FSM 
Elimination of Violence against Women policy a key outcome of work to date) , women in decision making, 
women’s economic empowerment, sexual and reproductive health and rights, (girl) child protection and 
gender mainstreaming, with links to other development partner initiatives and programs.  
The principal driving policy framework for the issue of food security and agriculture in FSM, the National 
Agriculture Policy, also highlights a specific goal for improving gender equity outcomes, namely to “Improve 
farm incomes and livelihoods with particular focus on gender and vulnerable groups.”  
Additional policy frameworks, laws and programmes supported include:  
• The FSM Constitution Article IV, section 4 ensures equal protection, which guarantees 
fundamental rights and freedom regardless of gender, sexual orientation, race, ancestry, national 
origin, religion, language, and social status.  
• FSM Environmental Protection Act (2012).  
• Kosrae State passed its Family Protection Act in 2014. Pohnpei State passed its Domestic 
Violence Act in 2017.   
• In 2019, Chuuk State Government raised the Age of Consent from age of 13 to 18 (C.S.L No. 6-
66).

provisions for the Yap State building codes. In Kosrae, State Law No. 8- 24 was amended to 
provide persons with disabilities better  accesses to government facilities and for other purposes.   
 
The FSM Gender Stocktake
4 in 2012 highlighted the following key issues for gender governance and 
mainstreaming gender into policy and legislative frameworks in FSM:  
• Budgets for the national and state programs cover only operational costs, including salary, travel 
and office suppli es, but no funds are allocated for development programs. Most development is 
through funds secured from development partners and the US Federal Funding system.  
• Participation of women at the highest levels of decision making remains very limited in FSM. 
Wom en continue to be absent or grossly under -represented at the legislative and executive levels 
of government.  
• Historically FSM is a matrilineal society where ancestral descent and land is passed down 
through maternal tribal lineage. In the modern age however, this historical lineage cannot be 
generalized. Currently, each family’s land agreements are differing from one family to another5. 
Sub-projects will be required to undertake a gender analysis and E&S screening to ensure these 
issues are embedded in complete risk analysis and community buy -in from both women and men 
is secured through multiple  entryways for stakeholder consultations through the EDA grant 
mechanism.    
• Other barriers to full participation in public decision making and participation at the higher 
government level include the lack of opportunity to undertake leadership training and the lack of 
support for women wanting to move into roles that have traditionally been dominated by men.  
• Lack of awareness  of the issue of gender equality  was highl ighted by FSM departments as a 
major barrier to higher political commitment to gender mainstreaming.  
• Lack of sex disaggregated data to inform policy developments  
The Department of Health and Social Affairs hosts the Gender Development Office ( GDO ) as the f ocal point 
for gender issues in FSM, and the  CEDAW Committee , with the FSM Gender Stocktake6 highlighted critical 
challenges, including:  
• GDO is not strategically located within the central government structure to influence the 
integration of gender across the policy, programming and resource allocation spectra.  
 
4 Stocktake of the gender mainstreaming capacity of Pacific Island governments –  FSM . Available at: 
https://www.spc.int/sites/default/files/wordpresscontent/wp- content/uploads/2017/03/web_2- FSM_gender_stocktake.pdf  
5 Discussions with women’s groups –  see Annex 1  
6 Stocktake of the gender mainstreaming capacity of Pacific Island governments –  FSM .

• GDO has few resources and no process to facilitate the mainstreaming of gender and women’s 
human rights across the whole of government.  
• GDO lacks expertise and experience in gender and human rights  analysis and integration, 
particularly when it comes to research and statistical support, gender -related planning and 
analysis, sub-grant  management, information management and dissemination and evaluation  
Further it should be noted that in addition to the GDO, FSM National government funds some gender 
positions, with Pohnpei and Yap having state- funded ‘women’s interest officers’ (or equivalent), but gender 
equality work in Chuuk and Kosrae is largely the responsibility of CSOs including the state women’s umbrella organizations.  These CSOs and women’s organizations will be engaged throughout the 
programme implementation and in particular through the sub- grant process, where community engagement 
is embedded in each step of the EDA grant facility (see  Section 11 of the feasibility study).  
Since the publication of the FSM Gender Stocktake, the Department of Health and Social Affairs  and GDO 
have  made significant progress including:  
• Actively engaging in policy, programming and resourcing spectra to inc orporate gender 
equality considerations in national government processes (for example the SDGs and the 
Voluntary National Review (V NR)), 
• Increased budget allocation for gender equality  
• The National E nding Violence Against Women  Policy and State Action Plans (action plans 
not yet endorsed)   
• Mobilized resources to support women’s economic empowerment , sexual reproductive health 
rights  and ending violence against women  
The Pacific Women Country Plan for FSM highlighted some progress in addressing these gaps, but many 
of them still remain significant barriers for FSM mainstreaming gender.
7 
The FSM Women’s Council  comprises office bearers from the four State women’s councils or associations, 
umbrella groups for all the women’s groups in the state: Chuuk Women’s Council (CWC), Kosrae Women’s Association (KWA), Pohnpei Women’s Council (PWC) and Yap Women’s Association (YWA). These 
women’s councils are key players in advocating for gender equality in their own states to support the six 
strategic goals in the National Gender Policy as well as implementing projects and programs identified by 
members. At the 2014 National Women’s Conference in Pohnpei, the women’s councils resolved to form 
an FSM Women’s Council. That body was legally established prior to the 2016 conference in Yap. The 
Women’s Councils completed the process to formally establish the FSM Women’s Council in January 2018 
and is now the coordinating body or national voice for women; and has received funding support from 
National Government through the GDO. The 2020 conference in Kosrae has been postponed and a key 
outcome of that meeting was the endorsement of the FSM Women’s Council Strategic Plan.  
The VNR notes that “it is in the civil society sector where women have an outsized impact on the collective 
consciousness and development of the many islands in FSM. More organized support and public -private 
and private- private partnerships should be sought as a matter of policy”.  
Women’s groups have been consulted in the development of the GAP and will continue to be part of the 
stakeholder engagement process as they are an integral part of communities across FSM.

The EDA programme will include  dedicated gender capacity support during programme implementation to 
ensure the GAP is carried out as well as ensure women’s groups and government gender officials are fully 
incorporated and engaged throughout the life of the EDA programme.  
Existing Gender Inequality – Statistical Overview  
The poverty profile for FSM highlights that at the national level about one- fifth of the population lives in 
female- headed households (17.8% -20.7% range across the states). These female- headed households are 
also on average larger and have more children ac ross all states except Yap. Further, at the national level 
over half of female- headed households live in poverty compared to 40% of male- headed households ( Table 
1).8

In 2013/2014, male- headed households across all states had higher average annual income than female-
headed households. At the national level, female- headed households earned 9% less on average than 
male- headed households, but that differential varied signifi cantly across states with male -headed 
households in Kosrae earning 2% more than female- headed households compared to a 42% differential in 
Chuuk. Breaking this down by income groups there isn’t much of a difference between male-  and female-
headed households earning less than USD  5,000 (37.4% of male households and 36.7% of female 
households), but the distribution of high- income households (> USD  30,000) shows higher percentage of 
male- headed households (10.2% vs. 6.7%) ( Table 2). 9

and female headed households saved 9% more than male headed households.10 While the expenditures 
breakdowns for male-  and female- headed households is pretty similar with food and non- alcoholic 
beverages accounting for 34.4% and 34.5% of monthly expenditures, respectively, there is a substantial 
difference in the level of expenditures with male- headed households spending on average 12% more than 
female- headed households.11 
In the workforce, similar disparities exist. Across the States, 62.7% (Chuuk) to 70.3% (Pohnpei) of the male population 15+ years old were in the w orkforce with 51% of that labor force engaged in formal work and 
48% engaged in home- based work (45% subsistence, 54% market -oriented). For females, only 43.3% 
(Chuuk) to 65.7% (Yap) of the population 15+ years old were in the workforce with 42% of that labor force 
engaged in formal work and 57% engaged in home- based work (58% subsistence, 41% market). Roughly 
a quarter of male and female households were employed in agriculture, but nearly 30% of females were employed in private households compared to just 10% of males. The other major differences are that 14% 
of males were employed in fishing compared to just 2% of females and 15% of males were employed in public administration compared to 9% of females. These differences highlight that income opportunities  for 
females in FSM are limited compared to those for men, and the income activities available to women tend 
to be more home- based work, particularly for subsistence.
12 
The gender differentials in labor force preparticipation are further evident in the FSM Social Security 
Administration statistics . In 2016, women represented 39% of Social Security contributors in FSM, and on 
average women social security contributors had 11% lower annual average gross earnings than their male 
counterparts. While this  ‘gender  earnings gap’  has reduced substantially over the last 20 years (19% since 
1997), it still highlights a significant issue to be addressed for gender equality. At the state level, Chuuk 
social security contributors were 40% female and the earnings gap was about 14%. For Pohnpei, 38% 
women and an 8% gap; for Yap, 43% women and an 11% gap; and for Kosrae 36% women and 16% gap. 
The VNR noted that women are leading transformative change through civil society. In the civil society 
sector, the FSM Social Security data shows that women’s participation is the same as men, and in addition 
they earn substantially more, on average, than men.  
For education, women in FSM have significantly lower educational attainment than men. At the elementary school level 69% of females  over 25 years and over  in FSM have completed at least elementary school 
compared to 75% of males, but this differential is more pronounced  in Yap and Kosrae where 90.2% and 
90.9% of males, respectively have completed elementary school compared t o only 68.5% and 78.8% of 
females, respectively. Overall,  about 40% of males have graduate high school compared to just 31% of 
females, but that differential is also more drastic in Yap and Kosrae where 72.9% and 68.2% of men had graduated high school, res pectively compared to 46.1% and 42.1% of women. The proportion of men with 
bachelor’s degrees (5.3%) is also about double the proportion of women with bachelor’s degrees (2.8%) at the national level ( Table 3).  
Further the 2012 Fertility Monograph for FSM further documents issues for gender inequality in FSM. In 2012, about 6% of women aged 15- 19 years had given birth , a relatively high rate of teenage fertility . 
Overall,  almost 100 births to women younger than 15 years of age were registered during the period 2000-
2010, and another 1,061 births of women aged 15- 17 years. The census data show that only a small 
proportion of teenage women who gave birth att ended school which creates additional hardships and lack 
of income potential in the future for the young women.

In the 2016 Agricultural Census, most of the people aged 15 and over in households with land used for 
agriculture had not graduated from high school – 58% of males and 59% of females. Twenty percent of 
males and 21% of females reported that they had graduated from high school, and 12%  of males and 11% 
of females reported some college education but no degree.15 
The FSM Education indicators Report  for 2018/2019 highlights that in 2019, net enrollment in FSM schools 
is 82% in elementary level, whereas it is only 65% in ECE and 46% Secondary level and while boys’ and girls’ net enrollment is almost equal in elementary level, boys’ NER is higher in EC E and girls’ NER is higher 
in secondary level. In grade 1, gross intake rate is slightly higher for male students compared to female  
students , whereas in grade 8 female gross intake rate is substantially higher than male gross intake rate. 
Possible reasons for this variation by gender could be associated with late entry of male in grade 1, whereas 
higher GIR for females in grade 8 could be associated with repetition in elementary level.  Most primary 
school survival rates throughout FSM are considered low with female students having slightly higher 
survival rates than male students. The data shows roughly ~45% (39% Male/50% Female) survival rate of cohort starting in Grade 1 and reaching Grade 8 and that same cohort starting and Grade 1 and reaching 
Grade 12 is very low at about ~20% (17% Male/25% Female).

The EDA programme should recognize the prevalence of discrimination and unequal participation of females 
in technical and vocational education and training, and ensur e women and girls are equitably represented in 
programme activities, and are recognized for their unique perspectives on DRR, food production, and water 
security. Table 6 below provides a more detailed breakdown of some of the key gender perspectives withi n 
the three thematic areas of the EDA programme; DRR, food security and water security.  
Women in Public Life and Decision- Making  
Until recently, women in FSM have been totally absent from the legislative and executive levels of 
government, and continue to be significantly under -represented in civic affairs today  (see Figure 1). In 2020,

there were three women serving as legislators in the Pohnpei State Legisl ature, and in Chuuk, there were 
two State female senators.  
In the public service, some women have achieved seniority and are able to influence government policies 
and programs. Statistics show that of all the major formal work sectors (national and state government, 
private sector and civil society), it is civil society where women actually earn more on average than men.  
 
Figure 1. Women in Political Leadership Positions in FSM States, 2020 Data (Source: VNR Report, 2020)  
There ar e no legal barriers to women’s representation in government, but there are significant socio- cultural 
restrictions. Traditionally, matrilineal societies enable women to actively participate in decision- making 
processes related to family, community and natural resource matters, but for the speaker of the family is male, usually an uncle or brother. The EDA  programme will ensure the participation of women in the 
decision -making process for the selection of adaption solutions within the three thematic areas of  the EDA 
Facility.  The active participation of women is embedded in the GAP below as well as in the programme’s 
Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7) . 
At the local level, women are involved in small businesses, cooperatives, within traditional structures a nd 
within faith -based organisations. Such women’s groups are organised and visible at community level. The  
nature and organization of such groups differ  considerably between groups of different types as well as 
from State to State and from community to community. This is because there is considerable complexity across different islands, communities and ethnic groups within FSM.  There is a Women’s Council in each 
of the four States . 
Agricultural Income  and Participation 
The 2016 FSM Agricultural Census provides a more detailed look at jobs and utilization in the agricultural sector  (crop raising, livestock, fisheries, forestry, and hunting)  which helps prov ide additional  context for 
gender disparities.  These considerations will need to be balanced by the sub- grant s, particularly those that 
are focused on food security . 
Just over 90% of households recorded in the Agriculture census had access to land they used for 
agriculture. Across FSM the householder was male in 80% of households. Yap had the highest proportion 
of female householders with access to land for agricultural activities (26%), followed by Pohnpei (21%), 
Chuuk (18%), and Kosrae (17%). In these households, 30% of males and 19% of females 15 years old or


---
*Page 124*
---

 
 
 
 
 
 
older reported paid employment in these fields, but this varied significantly by state : 44% of males and 28% 
of females in Yap, 18% of males and 12% of females in Chuuk, 39% of males and 23% of femal es in 
Pohnpei, and 41% of males and 24% of females in Kosrae. The farming activity main operators were mostly 
males (77%) and when the main operator was reported to be female, about 48% were over 55, compared 
to 32% of male main operators.  Where the main operator was female, nearly 60% received no income from 
agriculture, compared to 48% of households where the main operator was male.  It should be noted that 
subsistence farming tends to be a family enterprise with gendered division of labour, so the earning  
statistics should be understood in that context.  In specific agricultural sectors, the breakdowns are as 
follows:  
• Crop Raising  - 37% of the people participated with males making up 64.3% of the crop workers 
and females just 35.7%. However, this was flipped in some states as Yap had an estimated 62.4% of crop workers female and only 37.6% male.  
• Livestock  - Across FSM, 24% of people 15 and over worked on livestock activities . Gender 
distribution for livestock activities also varied across the states with females accounting for 55% of the workers in Yap, 36% in Chuuk, 31% in Pohnpei, and 14% in Kosrae.  
• Fishing/Aquaculture  - 18% of people over 15 participated in fishing activities with males 
dominating that industry with over 84% of the fishers. This varied ac ross states. In Yap, 5.5% of 
fishers were females, 16.5% in Chuuk, 20.2% in Pohnpei and 20.4% in Kosrae. In aquaculture, 
77% of the main operators and 70% of those that usually worked were males.  
• Forestry and Hunting  - Across FSM, 29% of males and 13% of females aged 15 and over were 
reported to be involved in forestry activities. In Yap, 44% of the people reported engaging in hunting activities were female, compared to 21% in Chuuk, 19% in Pohnpei and 24% in Kosrae.  
Women are involved in natural resour ce management, food security, and resilience initiatives  as stated 
above, through this EDA programme, women must be actively  engaged throughout the implementation of 
the programme. The GAP (below) provides specific activities to undertake to ensure active participation by 
both women and men.  
Violence Against Women  
In October 2014, FSM conducted a National Family Health and Safety Study17 which highlighted the 
major issue of violence against women in FSM including the following significant findings:  
• Almost o ne in three ever -partnered women in the FSM (32.8%) have experienced physical and/or 
sexual violence by a partner at least once in their life and slightly over 18% of ever -partnered 
women have experienced sexual violence by a partner in their lifetime.  
• Nearly one in four ever -partnered women (24.1%) experienced physical and/or sexual violence by 
a partner in the 12 months preceding the interview.  
• Two in five women who ever experienced partner violence (41%) were injured at least once in their lifetime due t o the violence.  
• More than one in three ever -abused women (35%) never told anyone about the violence. Those 
who did disclose it mostly confided in family members and friends. 89% of ever -abused women 
never went to formal services or authorities, such as health centers or police, for support. The most common reason for not seeking support from formal services or authorities was that 
respondents thought the violence was normal or not serious.  
• Almost 10% of all interviewed women experienced physical violence by a non- partner and the 
most common perpetrators were parents and other relatives.  
 
17 FSM National Health and Safety Report; Available at: https://pacific.unfpa.org/sites/default/files/pub- pdf/FSMFHSSReportweb.pdf


---
*Page 125*
---

 
 
 
 
 
 
• Children of women who experienced partner violence were almost three times more likely to have 
stopped or dropped out of school than children of women who never ex perienced partner 
violence  
Some key recommendations for FSM included that national and state level actions are implement ed to: 
• Promote a multi -sectoral coordination between the health system and other public agencies (e.g., 
legislature, judiciary, public s afety, social services) and private organizations (e.g., women’s 
groups, NGOs, private health centers) to address VAW in a comprehensive manner  
• Create shelters and other social services institutions with health, counseling, and security staff 
adequately tr ained to serve abused women and children. Locate those shelters close by a 
respected local leader to provide further security to abused women and children from the abusers.  
• Develop workshops for parents about parent -children relationships, gender roles, and gender 
equality  
• Promote the passing of the Family Protection Act in Pohnpei, Chuuk, and Yap.  
• Give funding priority to existing government programs aimed at addressing violence against 
women, such as the Domestic Violence Unit and the training program for  police officers on 
domestic violence.

Gender Mainstreaming Considerations for Sub- grant  Activities  
There are three principle sectors for the sub -grant s: disaster response/recovery, food security, water 
security.  Each underlying sub- grant  will have its own anticipated problem tree, but an overarching one 
specific to gender for the three principal  sectors is included below ( Figure  2).
